<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give an arithmetic classification of singular and absolutely continuous unbiased Bernoulli convolutions for every parameter $`\lambda\in(0,1)`$. The criterion is expressed through one-sided approximation by explicitly defined finite sets of algebraic units; it is an infinite approximation condition, not a finite membership algorithm. We also establish singular examples beyond reciprocals of Pisot numbers: singularity holds at the reciprocal of every quartic Salem number in $`(1,2)`$ and at the reciprocal of a specified non-Pisot root of an explicit degree-31 polynomial.

- **C1** (classification, [source](#stmt-cls-classification)): Define the parameter set $$\mathcal C=(0,1/2)\ \cup\ \left[(1/2,1)\cap \bigcup_{d\geq2}\ \bigcap_{j\geq1}\ \limsup_{n\to\infty} \bigcup_{r\in R_*(n,d,j)}[r-4^{-n},r)\right]. \label{cls:criterion}$$ For every $\lambda\in(0,1)$, the measure $\nu_\lambda$ is singular if and only if $\lambda\in\mathcal C$, and is absolutely continuous if and only if $\lambda\notin\mathcal C$. Replacing $\limsup$ by $\liminf$ in <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a> gives the same set.
  - Explanation: An arithmetic approximation condition characterizes the measure type of every unbiased Bernoulli convolution.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cls-pure-type)): For $0<\lambda<1$, the measure $\mu_\lambda$ is either singular or absolutely continuous. It is singular for $\lambda<1/2$ and uniform on $[0,2]$ for $\lambda=1/2$. Consequently, $\nu_{1/2}$ is uniform on $[-2,2]$.
  - Explanation: Pure type and the baseline parameter regimes separate singularity from absolute continuity.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cls-compact-concentration)): A Borel probability measure $\rho$ on $\mathbb R$ is singular if and only if, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a compact set $K$ with $$\operatorname{Leb}(K)<\varepsilon, \qquad \rho(K)>1-\varepsilon.$$
  - Explanation: Singularity is equivalent to compact concentration with arbitrarily small Lebesgue measure.
- **C4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cls-first-bit-overlap)): There is a finite measure $\xi\ne0$ with $\xi\leq\mu_0$ and $\xi\leq\mu_1$.
  - Explanation: The subprobability measures obtained by fixing the first bit have nonzero common mass.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-cls-cross-pairs)): If $\mu_\lambda$ is singular, there are constants $a\in(0,L/2)$ and $c>0$, depending only on $\lambda$, with the following property. For every $Q>1$ and all sufficiently large $n$, at least $c2^n$ words $u\in\{0,1\}^n$ with $u_0=0$ each have at least $Q(2\lambda)^n$ distinct neighbors $v\in\{0,1\}^n$ such that $$v_0=1, \qquad \|s_u-s_v\|\leq(L-a)\lambda^n. \label{cls:cross-pair-bound}$$ The constants $a,c$ are independent of $Q$ and $n$.
  - Explanation: Singularity forces many close prefix pairs with opposite first bits.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-cls-finite-order)): Fix $\lambda\in(0,1)$. There are an integer $M\geq1$ and constants $c_0,x_0>0$ such that every real polynomial $p(t)=-1+\sum_{k\geq1}a_kt^k$ with $\|a_k\|\leq1$ satisfies $$\sup_{t\in[\lambda,\lambda+x]}\|p(t)\|\geq c_0x^M \qquad(0<x<x_0).$$
  - Explanation: Bounded polynomial coefficients and a fixed constant term give a uniform finite-order nonvanishing estimate.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-cls-prescribed-prime)): Fix integers $D\geq2$ and $d>2D$. For all sufficiently large $n$, every polynomial $F\in\mathbb F_2[t]$ with $\deg F<Dn$ and $F(0)=1$ has a monic irreducible completion $P\in\mathbb F_2[t]$ satisfying $$\deg P=dn, \qquad P\equiv F+t^{Dn}\pmod{t^{Dn+1}}.$$ The threshold in $n$ is uniform in $F$.
  - Explanation: Prescribed initial coefficients admit irreducible completions over the binary field at sufficiently large degrees.
- **C8** (construction, [source](#stmt-cls-realization)): Fix $\lambda\in(1/2,1)$ and $a\in(0,L)$, where $L=(1-\lambda)^{-1}$. There is an integer $d_0$ such that, for each integer $d\geq d_0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, the following assertion holds uniformly over $w\in\{-1,0,1\}^n$. If $$w_0=-1, \qquad \left\|\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_i\lambda^i\right\| \leq(L-a)\lambda^n, \label{cls:realization-hypothesis}$$ then some $r\in R(n,d)$ satisfies $$\lambda<r\leq\lambda+\tfrac12 4^{-n}, \qquad \pi_n(p_r)=w.$$
  - Explanation: A sufficiently small prefix evaluation can be realized by a nearby algebraic unit strictly above the parameter.
- **C9** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cls-garsia)): Let $\beta\in(1,2)$ be an algebraic integer of absolute norm $2$ whose conjugates all have modulus greater than $1$. Then $1/\beta\notin \mathcal C$, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is absolutely continuous.
  - Explanation: The norm separation argument gives absolute continuity for the specified algebraic parameters.
- **C10** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cls-pisot)): If $\beta\in(1,2)$ is a Pisot number, then $1/\beta\in\mathcal C$, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular.
  - Explanation: Reciprocal Pisot parameters yield singular convolutions and satisfy the arithmetic criterion.
- **C11** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sal-main)): Let $\beta\in(1,2)$ be a Salem number of degree four. Then the unbiased Bernoulli convolution $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.
  - Explanation: Reciprocals of quartic Salem numbers in the stated interval yield singular convolutions.
- **C12** (construction, [source](#stmt-sal-construction)): There are positive integers $k_m$ and a constant $\gamma>0$ for which the numbers $L_m$ in <a href="#sal:multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:multipliers">[sal:multipliers]</a> are positive and satisfy the following properties. Set $$D=\frac{10}{\log\beta},\qquad K_m=\left\lceil2b_m+DL_m\right\rceil\quad(m\ge2).$$ Then $A_m\ge1$ for every $m$, and $$\begin{aligned} K_m\le k_{m+1}\le e^{1.9L_m},\qquad L_{m+1}&\ge1.75L_m &&(m\ge2),\label{sal:growth-initial}\\ \log K_m\le .75L_m,\qquad L_{m+1}&\ge2.1L_m &&(m\ge3).\label{sal:growth} \end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\label{sal:cosine-floor} \|\cos(\pi u_{m,j})\|\ge\gamma \quad(m\ge1,\ j\in\mathbb Z),$$ and, for $m\ge2$, $$\label{sal:small-errors} 2\delta_m\le\frac{\gamma}{2\pi},\qquad \beta^{b_m-k_{m+1}}\le e^{-10L_m} \le\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}.$$
  - Explanation: Algebraic multipliers have small unit-circle conjugates, controlled growth, and a uniform cosine floor.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-sal-loss)): For the sequence in Lemma <a href="#sal:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:construction">12</a>, $S_m=o(L_m)$. Furthermore, with $\eta=.01$, for $m\ge2$ and $n\ge0$, $$\label{sal:total-loss} \sum_{j\le n}f(u_{m,j}) \le(2+\eta)S_m+Cn\delta_m^2.$$
  - Explanation: The logarithmic cosine loss is asymptotically smaller than the reciprocal-conjugate growth scale.
- **C14** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sal-examples)): Let $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$ be the respective real roots greater than one of $$P_1(x)=x^4-x^3-x^2-x+1, \qquad P_2(x)=x^4-2x^3+x^2-2x+1.$$ Then $\beta_1,\beta_2\in(1,2)$ and both $\nu_{1/\beta_1}$ and $\nu_{1/\beta_2}$ are singular.
  - Explanation: The explicit quartic polynomials give Salem parameters with singular convolutions.
- **C15** (theorem, [source](#stmt-np-main)): The polynomial $p$ in <a href="#np:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:polynomial">[np:polynomial]</a> has a unique real root $\beta\in(1.8392,1.8393)$. This root is not a Pisot number, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.
  - Explanation: The specified polynomial has a non-Pisot real root whose reciprocal convolution is singular.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-root-data)): All roots of $p$ are simple. Its roots outside the unit circle are $\beta,\alpha,\overline\alpha$, where $\beta$ is real, $\operatorname{Im}\alpha>0$, and $$\begin{gathered} 1.8392<\beta<1.8393,\qquad 1.0002089<\|\alpha\|^2<1.0002094,\\ \|\alpha\|^{-1}<0.9999. \end{gathered} \label{np:root-bounds}$$ The remaining roots are fourteen nonreal conjugate pairs, each of modulus less than $0.994$.
  - Explanation: Root enclosures separate the expanding roots from the contracting conjugate pairs.
- **C17** (construction, [source](#stmt-np-lattice)): All vectors $y(a)$, for all $N$ and all words $a$, belong to a fixed full lattice in this real vector space. Their contracting coordinates $(y_r)_{r\in I}$ and normalized expanding coordinates $(x_r)_{r\in D}$ lie in fixed bounded boxes. The change from $(x_D,y_I)$ to $(y_D,y_I)$ has real Jacobian determinant of absolute value $\mathcal M^N$.
  - Explanation: Word evaluations lie in a fixed full lattice with bounded normalized coordinates and a specified volume expansion.
- **C18** (theorem, [source](#stmt-np-word-character)): If $b_k=a_{N-1-k}$, then for $A\in\{0,1\}$ and $i\in\mathbb Z$, $$Z_i^{(A,N)}(x(a)) =\exp\!\left(\pi\mathrm i\sum_{k=0}^{N-1} C_A(k-i)(2b_k-1)\right). \label{np:word-character-formula}$$
  - Explanation: Characters at word vectors equal phases formed from reversed bits and Laurent coefficients.
- **C19** (construction, [source](#stmt-np-moment-bounds)): There exist weights $w_{ks}\in10^{-9}\mathbb Z$, indexed by $30\le k\le2000$ and $s\in\{0,1\}$, such that $$0.001+0.0055+\sum_{k,s}\|w_{ks}\| =\frac{198442946}{10^9}<x_*:=0.199 \label{np:weight-sum}$$ and, in the stationary model, $$\begin{gathered} \mathbb E\mathcal L_i>0.000210,\qquad \mathbb E\|\mathcal G_i\|^2<0.000145,\\ \left\|\mathbb E\bigl[(\mathcal Z_i^{(0)})^2\mathcal G_i^2\bigr]\right\| <0.000007. \end{gathered} \label{np:moment-estimates}$$ In particular, $\|G_i^{(N)}(x)\|\le x_*$ for every $N,i,x$, and $\|\mathcal G_i\|\le x_*$ for every bit sequence.
  - Explanation: Rational predictor weights satisfy correlation, norm, and anisotropy bounds.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-damping-bounds)): Set $$S_*=\left(1+\frac{2x_*^4}{1-x_*^2}\right)^{18} \left(1+\frac{2x_*^3}{1-x_*^2}\right)-1. \label{np:pattern-cost}$$ Then $S_*<0.081$, and $$\begin{gathered} \sum_{\substack{d\text{ admissible}\\o(d)>0}}x_*^{o(d)}\le S_*, \qquad \eta_i x_*^2\le S_*/3,\\ \frac{\overline\eta}{1-S_*/3}<0.101, \qquad \overline\eta=\frac1{21}\sum_{i\in B_q}\eta_i. \end{gathered} \label{np:damping-estimates}$$
  - Explanation: Admissible-pattern costs control the damping coefficients.
- **C21** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-integral)): For every $N$ and every fixed complex coordinate $x_\alpha$, $$\int_\mathbb R\psi(x_\beta)\prod_{i\in J_N}F_i(x_D)\,dx_\beta \le\int_\mathbb R\psi(x_\beta)\,dx_\beta, \qquad \psi(v)=\left(\frac{\sin(\pi v/10)}{\pi v/10}\right)^2, \label{np:integral-bound}$$ where $\psi(0)=1$.
  - Explanation: The positive trigonometric product has a uniformly bounded integral against the specified test function.
- **C22** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-log-inequality)): For $0\le x\le x_*$ and $\|u\|\le2x$, $$\log(1+(1-x^2)u)\ge u-\frac{u^2}{2}-C_*x^3, \qquad C_*:=\frac2{3\sqrt{1-x_*^2}}. \label{np:log-bound}$$
  - Explanation: A logarithmic estimate controls the cubic remainder in the positive-factor gain.
- **C23** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-typical-gain)): For uniformly distributed words $a\in\{0,1\}^N$, $$\Pr\left\{\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x(a))>0.000220N\right\} \longrightarrow1. \label{np:typical-gain-bound}$$
  - Explanation: The logarithmic product grows at the stated rate on a set of words with probability tending to certainty.
- **C24** (bound, [source](#stmt-np-thickening)): There are constants $\rho>0$ and $C_{\mathrm{thick}}<\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href="#np:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:lattice">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, $$\left\|\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x'_D) -\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x_D(a))\right\|\le C_{\mathrm{thick}}, \qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\quad(r\in D). \label{np:thickening-bound}$$
  - Explanation: Disjoint lattice-point thickenings change the total logarithmic product by a bounded amount.

      "exact_statement": "Fix $\\lambda\\in(1/2,1)$ and $a\\in(0,L)$, where $L=(1-\\lambda)^{-1}$. If $$w_0=-1,\n  \\qquad\n  \\left|\\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_i\\lambda^i\\right|\n      \\leq(L-a)\\lambda^n,\n  \\label{cls:realization-hypothesis}$$ then some $r\\in R(n,d)$ satisfies $$\\lambda<r\\leq\\lambda+\\tfrac12 4^{-n},\n  \\qquad \\pi_n(p_r)=w.$$",
      "explanation": "A sufficiently small prefix evaluation can be realized by a nearby algebraic unit strictly above the parameter.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {

          "source_id": "PM-153.1"
        }

        "exact_quote": "There are positive integers $k_m$ and a constant $\\gamma>0$ for which the numbers $L_m$ in <a href=\"#sal:multipliers\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sal:multipliers\">[sal:multipliers]</a> are positive and satisfy the following properties. Set $$D=\\frac{10}{\\log\\beta},\\qquad\n K_m=\\left\\lceil2b_m+DL_m\\right\\rceil\\quad(m\\ge2).$$ Then $A_m\\ge1$ for every $m$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n K_m\\le k_{m+1}\\le e^{1.9L_m},\\qquad\n L_{m+1}&\\ge1.75L_m &&(m\\ge2),\\label{sal:growth-initial}\\\\\n \\log K_m\\le .75L_m,\\qquad\n L_{m+1}&\\ge2.1L_m &&(m\\ge3).\\label{sal:growth} \n\\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\\label{sal:cosine-floor}\n |\\cos(\\pi u_{m,j})|\\ge\\gamma\n       \\quad(m\\ge1,\\ j\\in\\mathbb Z),$$ and, for $m\\ge2$, $$\\label{sal:small-errors}\n 2\\delta_m\\le\\frac{\\gamma}{2\\pi},\\qquad\n \\beta^{b_m-k_{m+1}}\\le e^{-10L_m}\n                       \\le\\frac{\\gamma}{2\\pi}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-153.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sal-construction",
          "exact_quote": "There are positive integers $k_m$ and a constant $\\gamma>0$ for which the numbers $L_m$ in <a href=\"#sal:multipliers\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"sal:multipliers\">[sal:multipliers]</a> are positive and satisfy the following properties.           "exact_quote": "<span id=\"np:roots\" label=\"np:roots\"></span>All roots of $p$ are simple. Its roots outside the unit circle are $\\beta,\\alpha,\\overline\\alpha$, where $\\beta$ is real, $\\operatorname{Im}\\alpha>0$, and $$\\begin{gathered}\n  1.8392<\\beta<1.8393,\\qquad\n  1.0002089<|\\alpha|^2<1.0002094,\\\\\n  |\\alpha|^{-1}<0.9999.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\label{np:root-bounds}$$ The remaining roots are fourteen nonreal conjugate pairs, each of modulus less than $0.994$.",
          "source_id": "PM-153.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-np-root-data"
    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-np-lattice",
        "exact_quote": "All vectors $y(a)$, for all $N$ and all words $a$, belong to a fixed full lattice in this real vector space. Their contracting coordinates $(y_r)_{r\\in I}$ and normalized expanding coordinates $(x_r)_{r\\in D}$ lie in fixed bounded boxes. The change from $(x_D,y_I)$ to $(y_D,y_I)$ has real Jacobian determinant of absolute value $\\mathcal M^N$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-np-moment-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "<span id=\"np:moments\" label=\"np:moments\"></span>There exist weights $w_{ks}\\in10^{-9}\\mathbb Z$, indexed by $30\\le k\\le2000$ and $s\\in\\{0,1\\}$, such that $$0.001+0.0055+\\sum_{k,s}|w_{ks}|\n   =\\frac{198442946}{10^9}<x_*:=0.199\n \\label{np:weight-sum}$$ and, in the stationary model, $$\\begin{gathered}\n \\mathbb E\\mathcal L_i>0.000210,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E|\\mathcal G_i|^2<0.000145,\\\\\n \\left|\\mathbb E\\bigl[(\\mathcal Z_i^{(0)})^2\\mathcal G_i^2\\bigr]\\right|\n     <0.000007.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\label{np:moment-estimates}$$ In particular, $|G_i^{(N)}(x)|\\le x_*$ for every $N,i,x$, and $|\\mathcal G_i|\\le x_*$ for every bit sequence.",
        "source_id": "PM-153.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-np-damping-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "Set $$S_*=\\left(1+\\frac{2x_*^4}{1-x_*^2}\\right)^{18}\n       \\left(1+\\frac{2x_*^3}{1-x_*^2}\\right)-1.\n \\label{np:pattern-cost}$$ Then $S_*<0.081$, and $$\\begin{gathered}\n \\sum_{\\substack{d\\text{ admissible}\\\\o(d)>0}}x_*^{o(d)}\\le S_*,\n \\qquad \\eta_i x_*^2\\le S_*/3,\\\\\n \\frac{\\overline\\eta}{1-S_*/3}<0.101,\n \\qquad \\overline\\eta=\\frac1{21}\\sum_{i\\in B_q}\\eta_i.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\label{np:damping-estimates}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-153.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-np-thickening",
        "exact_quote": "There are constants $\\rho>0$ and $C_{\\mathrm{thick}}<\\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href=\"#np:lattice\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"np:lattice\">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, $$\\left|\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x'_D)\n       -\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x_D(a))\\right|\\le C_{\\mathrm{thick}},\n \\qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\\quad(r\\in D).\n \\label{np:thickening-bound}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-153.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-153.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "There are constants $\\rho>0$ and $C_{\\mathrm{thick}}<\\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href=\"#np:lattice\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"np:lattice\">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, $$\\left|\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x'_D)\n       -\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x_D(a))\\right|\\le C_{\\mathrm{thick}},\n \\qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\\quad(r\\in D).\n \\label{np:thickening-bound}$$",

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-np-thickening",
          "exact_quote": "There are constants $\\rho>0$ and $C_{\\mathrm{thick}}<\\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href=\"#np:lattice\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"np:lattice\">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, $$\\left|\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x'_D)\n       -\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x_D(a))\\right|\\le C_{\\mathrm{thick}},\n \\qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\\quad(r\\in D).\n \\label{np:thickening-bound}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-153.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "C24",
      "kind": "bound",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-np-thickening",
          "exact_quote": "There are constants $\\rho>0$ and $C_{\\mathrm{thick}}<\\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href=\"#np:lattice\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"np:lattice\">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, $$\\left|\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x'_D)\n       -\\sum_{i\\in J_N}\\log F_i(x_D(a))\\right|\\le C_{\\mathrm{thick}},\n \\qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\\quad(r\\in D).\n \\label{np:thickening-bound}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-153.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-np-thickening"
    }

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Unbiased Bernoulli convolution** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $0<\lambda<1$, the unbiased Bernoulli convolution $\nu_\lambda$ is the law of $$\label{intro:series} Y_\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_n\lambda^n, \qquad \mathbb P(\varepsilon_n=1)=\mathbb P(\varepsilon_n=-1)=\tfrac12,$$ where the signs are independent.
- **Pisot number** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *Pisot number* is a real algebraic integer $\beta>1$ whose other conjugates have modulus less than one.
- **Salem number** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *Salem number* is a real algebraic integer $\beta>1$ whose other conjugates consist of $\beta^{-1}$ and conjugates on the unit circle.
- **Algebraic unit targets** ([source](#cls:section-criterion)): For integers $n\geq 1$ and $d\geq 2$, let $R(n,d)$ be the set of real algebraic units $r\in(1/2,1)$ of degree $dn$ whose minimal polynomial has all coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$ and is irreducible modulo $2$.
- **Projection cluster** ([source](#cls:section-criterion)): For $r\in R(n,d)$, define the finite set $$W_n(r;d)= \left\{\pi_n(p_q):q\in R(n,d),\ \|q-r\|\leq 4^{-n}\right\}. \label{cls:projection-set}$$
- **Closed orthant** ([source](#cls:retained-roots)): For a bit word $u=(u_0,\ldots,u_{n-1})\in\{0,1\}^n$, let $$\mathcal O_u= \{w\in\mathbb R^n:(2u_i-1)w_i\geq0\text{ for }0\leq i<n\}$$ be the associated closed orthant.
- **Retained targets** ([source](#cls:retained-roots)): For $j\geq1$, put $$R_*(n,d,j)= \left\{r\in R(n,d): \#\left\{u\in\{0,1\}^n: \#(W_n(r;d)\cap\mathcal O_u)\geq j(2r)^n \right\}\geq\frac{2^n}{d}\right\}. \label{cls:retained-roots}$$
- **Word coordinates** ([source](#np:word-coordinates)): For a word $a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{N-1})\in\{0,1\}^N$, define $$y_r(a)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_jr^j\quad(r\in D\cup I), \qquad x_r(a)=r^{-N}y_r(a)\quad(r\in D). \label{np:word-coordinates}$$
- **Stationary phases** ([source](#np:stationary)): The corresponding stationary model uses independent fair bits $(b_k)_{k\in\mathbb Z}$ and the unit random variables $$\mathcal Z_i^{(A)}= \exp\!\left(\pi\mathrm i\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}C_A(k-i)(2b_k-1)\right). \label{np:stationary}$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-cls-classification) / [proof](#cls:subsection-forward): Coefficient-vector crowding forces concentration. -> Singularity
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-cls-classification) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Concentration supplies opposite-first-bit pairs; polynomial completion supplies one-sided approximants. -> Arithmetic approximation criterion
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-cls-garsia) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cls-garsia): Algebraic norms separate distinct prefix evaluations. -> Absolute continuity
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-cls-pisot) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cls-pisot): Trace estimates give Fourier nondecay. -> Singularity
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-sal-main) / [proof](#sal:concentration): Independent phase windows distinguish the convolution from Lebesgue measure. -> Singularity
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-np-root-data) / [proof](#cert:roots): Rational center inequalities and a boundary remainder estimate isolate every root. -> Root bounds
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-np-moment-bounds) / [proof](#cert:algorithm): Rounded rational recurrences and analytic tail errors control the predictor moments. -> Predictor estimates
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-np-main) / [proof](#np:cover-section): A bounded integral and typical gain remove enough lattice volume to produce a null cover. -> Singularity

Replacing $\\limsup$ by $\\liminf$ in <a href=\"#cls:criterion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cls:criterion\">[cls:criterion]</a> gives the same set.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-pattern-cost**: The weighted sum of admissible nonzero digit patterns is bounded by the stated pattern cost; the stated damping ratio is bounded as well.
- **I-infinite-classification**: The arithmetic classification criterion uses infinite one-sided approximation and does not give a finite decision procedure for arbitrary real inputs.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-finite-classifier**: Treat finite polynomial enumeration or a finite pattern-cost check as a measure-type decision procedure. -> A finite check cannot establish the infinite approximation condition for an arbitrary real parameter.
- **A-fourier-decay**: Infer absolute continuity from Fourier decay alone. -> The stated Salem examples are singular even though their Fourier transform tends to zero.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The admissible digit patterns and their rational weighted costs form a finite construction.

```python
# INPUTS: n numerator denominator on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: integer 0 <= n <= 21; denominator > 0; 0 <= numerator/denominator <= 199/1000.
# OUTPUTS: exact nonzero admissible-pattern weight sum as numerator/denominator.
# TIME: O(n) rational operations on seven states.
# SPACE: O(1) rational state entries; integer sizes grow with n and input size.
# ARITHMETIC: python-flint fmpq exact rational arithmetic.
# FAILURE: malformed or out-of-contract input raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import sys
from flint import fmpq
n, a, b = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
assert 0 <= n <= 21 and b > 0 and 0 <= 1000*a <= 199*b
x = fmpq(a,b)
# States: zero, A+, B+, C+, A-, B-, C-.
edges = [(0,0,0),(0,1,1),(1,2,1),(2,3,1),(3,1,0),(3,0,1),
         (0,4,1),(4,5,1),(5,6,1),(6,4,0),(6,0,1)]
v = [fmpq(1)] + [fmpq(0) for _ in range(6)]
for _ in range(n):
    w = [fmpq(0) for _ in range(7)]
    for src, dst, nonzero in edges:
        w[dst] += v[src] * (x if nonzero else 1)
    v = w
cost = v[0] + v[3] + v[6] - 1
print(str(cost.numerator) + '/' + str(cost.denominator))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (nonconstructive): Finite target enumeration does not give a finite membership test for arbitrary real parameters. Blocked use: ['Do not decide the infinite approximation criterion from a finite search.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The classification concerns unbiased Bernoulli convolutions with independent signs. Blocked use: ['Do not transfer the conclusions to biased or dependent sign laws without a separate argument.']
- **L3** (edge_case): The boundary parameter has a uniform law and must be separated from the singular regime below it. Blocked use: ['Do not include the boundary parameter in the singular interval below it.']
- **L4** (counterexample_scope): The Salem singularity theorem assumes degree four and the stated real interval. Blocked use: ['Do not extend this Salem conclusion to arbitrary degree.']
- **L5** (hypothesis): The Garsia conclusion requires the absolute norm and every conjugate modulus hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Do not infer absolute continuity from algebraicity alone.']
- **L6** (approximation): Full dimension alone does not establish absolute continuity. Blocked use: ['Do not replace a measure-type proof by a dimension conclusion.']
- **L7** (approximation): Fourier decay alone does not decide absolute continuity for the Salem parameters under discussion. Blocked use: ['Do not classify these parameters by Fourier decay alone.']
- **L8** (approximation): The moment certificate requires analytic control of its infinite tails as well as finite rational products. Blocked use: ['Do not certify the infinite moments from truncated products without their error bounds.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): The predictor weights follow the prescribed recurrence and rounding construction. Blocked use: ['Do not substitute arbitrary fitted weights while retaining the stated moment guarantees.']
- **L10** (formal_scope): Checking the finite arithmetic certificate alone does not discharge the analytic and measure-theoretic singularity argument. Blocked use: ['Do not present selected arithmetic checks as verification of the whole singularity theorem.']
- **L11** (unknown): The displayed automaton source and its visual witness disagree about a return transition. A finite implementation must state which transition description it follows. Blocked use: ['Do not claim that the visual witness and displayed transition source are interchangeable.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give an arithmetic classification of singular and absolutely continuous unbiased Bernoulli convolutions for every parameter $`\lambda\in(0,1)`$. The criterion is expressed through one-sided approximation by explicitly defined finite sets of algebraic units; it is an infinite approximation condition, not a finite membership algorithm. We also establish singular examples beyond reciprocals of Pisot numbers: singularity holds at the reciprocal of every quartic Salem number in $`(1,2)`$ and at the reciprocal of a specified non-Pisot root of an explicit degree-31 polynomial.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For $0<\lambda<1$, the unbiased Bernoulli convolution $\nu_\lambda$ is the law of <a id="intro:series"></a>$$\label{intro:series}
 Y_\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_n\lambda^n,
 \qquad \mathbb P(\varepsilon_n=1)=\mathbb P(\varepsilon_n=-1)=\tfrac12,$$ where the signs are independent. The series converges absolutely. We also use the bit form $\mu_\lambda$, the law of $$X_\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_n\lambda^n,
 \qquad \mathbb P(u_n=0)=\mathbb P(u_n=1)=\tfrac12.$$ The relation $Y_\lambda=2X_\lambda-(1-\lambda)^{-1}$ preserves singularity and absolute continuity. A measure is singular if it gives full mass to a Borel set of Lebesgue measure zero. Bernoulli convolutions have pure type: they are either singular or absolutely continuous (Jessen and Wintner 1935, Theorem 11) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[JessenWintner1935](#ref-JessenWintner1935)]. The classification problem asks which of these alternatives holds for each parameter, including exceptional parameters.

For $\lambda<1/2$, a direct interval cover proves singularity; at $\lambda=1/2$, $\nu_\lambda$ is uniform on $[-2,2]$. The interval $(1/2,1)$ presents the arithmetic difficulty. A *Pisot number* is a real algebraic integer $\beta>1$ whose other conjugates have modulus less than one. Erdős proved singularity when $\lambda^{-1}$ is Pisot by showing that the Fourier transform does not tend to zero (Erdős 1939) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Erdos1939](#ref-Erdos1939)]. Garsia subsequently established absolute continuity for a different arithmetic class, including reciprocals of algebraic integers of absolute norm two whose conjugates all have modulus greater than one (Garsia 1962) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Garsia1962](#ref-Garsia1962)].

The almost-everywhere theory gives a complementary picture. Solomyak proved that $\nu_\lambda$ has an $L^2$ density for Lebesgue-almost every $\lambda\in(1/2,1)$ (Solomyak 1995) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Solomyak1995](#ref-Solomyak1995)]. Hochman’s work relates dimension loss to very strong concentration of cylinder positions (Hochman 2014) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Hochman2014](#ref-Hochman2014)], and Shmerkin proved that the possible singular parameters in this interval form a set of Hausdorff dimension zero (Shmerkin 2014) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Shmerkin2014](#ref-Shmerkin2014)]. Varjú proved full dimension for every transcendental parameter in this interval (Varjú 2019) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[VarjuTranscendental2019](#ref-VarjuTranscendental2019)]. These conclusions leave measure type at exceptional parameters to be resolved; full dimension alone does not imply absolute continuity. The survey (Peres, Schlag, and Solomyak 2000) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[PeresSchlagSolomyak2000](#ref-PeresSchlagSolomyak2000)] describes the classical development and the roles of arithmetic, overlap, and Fourier decay.

Garsia connected singularity with entropy of finite sums (Garsia 1963) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Garsia1963](#ref-Garsia1963)]. Algebraic approximation also plays a central role in the dimension theory: Breuillard and Varjú showed that a dimension-deficient parameter can be approximated exceptionally closely by algebraic parameters whose Bernoulli convolutions also have dimension less than one (Breuillard and Varjú 2019, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BreuillardVarju2019](#ref-BreuillardVarju2019)]. The criterion below addresses measure type and also records the number and coefficient directions of nearby algebraic targets.

A *Salem number* is a real algebraic integer $\beta>1$ whose other conjugates consist of $\beta^{-1}$ and conjugates on the unit circle. In degree four there is a single nonreal conjugate pair. Salem proved that the Fourier transform of $\nu_\lambda$ tends to zero whenever $\lambda^{-1}$ is not Pisot (Salem 1943) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Salem1943](#ref-Salem1943)]. Thus Fourier nondecay cannot prove singularity for Salem parameters. Marshall-Maldonado and Solomyak recently obtained quantitative decay estimates for these convolutions (Marshall-Maldonado and Solomyak 2026, Appendix B) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[MarshallMaldonadoSolomyak2026](#ref-MarshallMaldonadoSolomyak2026)]; those estimates do not decide absolute continuity. The existence of singular examples with non-Pisot reciprocal is explicitly recorded as open in the recent literature (Baker et al. 2026; Kern and Sterk 2026) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BakerEtAl2026](#ref-BakerEtAl2026), [KernSterk2026](#ref-KernSterk2026)].

This paper gives an arithmetic criterion for every parameter and proves two classes of non-Pisot singularity results. The criterion in Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a> uses finite sets of real algebraic units. Their minimal polynomials have coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$, prescribed degree, and irreducible reduction modulo two. A target unit is retained when nearby units supply sufficiently many distinct initial coefficient vectors in a fixed positive proportion of the coordinate orthants. Singularity is equivalent to one-sided approximation by these retained targets at a fixed geometric rate. All target sets are defined by finite polynomial algebra; their construction uses no information about the type of any Bernoulli convolution. The resulting classification is an approximation property, and need not provide a short test for membership at a given real parameter.

The main step behind the criterion is a realization lemma. Concentration of the measure produces many pairs of finite bit words with nearby sums and different first bits. Their difference vectors can be completed to height-one polynomials having a root strictly above the parameter. A uniform finite-order nonvanishing estimate preserves a sign change, while prime polynomials in arithmetic progressions over $\mathbb F_2$ supply irreducibility without altering the initial coefficients. This separates the concentration argument from the algebraic realization, and gives a way to translate overlap information into constrained algebraic approximation.

The two non-Pisot results are proved directly; the criterion then yields the corresponding approximation properties. Theorem <a href="#sal:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:main">11</a> proves singularity for the reciprocal of every quartic Salem number in $(1,2)$. We construct algebraic multipliers whose unit-circle conjugates become small faster than the associated cosine losses accumulate. Trace identities then localize many Fourier phases to disjoint windows of random signs. Their averages concentrate under the Bernoulli convolution while tending to zero in Lebesgue mean square. This yields singularity even though the Fourier transform tends to zero. The construction applies in particular to the two quartic polynomials specified in Corollary <a href="#sal:examples" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:examples">14</a>.

Theorem <a href="#np:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:main">15</a> treats a root of an explicit degree-$31$ polynomial with a complex-conjugate pair outside the unit circle. Here word evaluations lie in a lattice whose box volume grows slightly faster than the reciprocal parameter to the word length. A product of positive trigonometric factors has bounded Lebesgue integral but grows exponentially on typical word evaluations. The resulting saving outweighs the extra volume growth. A finite-state description of nearly cancelling frequencies controls the integral; an explicit rational calculation verifies the required moment inequalities. Appendix <a href="#cert:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:section">7</a> provides the root enclosures, finite recurrences, and analytic error bounds for that calculation.

Sections <a href="#cls:section-criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:section-criterion">2</a>–<a href="#cls:section-classical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:section-classical">4</a> prove the classification and recover the basic Pisot and Garsia cases. Sections <a href="#sal:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:section">5</a> and <a href="#np:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:section">6</a> establish the two non-Pisot results. All logarithms are natural unless a base is specified, and all decimal constants used in estimates denote exact rational numbers.

<a id="cls:section-criterion"></a>
### An arithmetic criterion for the measure type

The criterion in this section is expressed through finite sets of algebraic units and the coefficient vectors of their minimal polynomials. Its proof has two parts. A sufficiently large cluster of coefficient vectors forces concentration of the Bernoulli convolution. Conversely, concentration produces many short polynomial prefixes, each of which can be completed to an irreducible polynomial with a root just above the parameter.

For integers $n\geq 1$ and $d\geq 2$, let $R(n,d)$ be the set of real algebraic units $r\in(1/2,1)$ of degree $dn$ whose minimal polynomial has all coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$ and is irreducible modulo $2$. An algebraic unit here means an algebraic integer with algebraic-integer inverse. Write $p_r$ for the minimal polynomial with its sign chosen so that its constant coefficient is $-1$, and set $$\pi_n(p_r)=([t^0]p_r,\ldots,[t^{n-1}]p_r)\in\{-1,0,1\}^n.$$ For $r\in R(n,d)$, define the finite set <a id="cls:projection-set"></a>$$W_n(r;d)=
  \left\{\pi_n(p_q):q\in R(n,d),\ |q-r|\leq 4^{-n}\right\}.
  \label{cls:projection-set}$$ This is a set of vectors: repeated projections, including those arising from conjugate roots of one polynomial, are counted only once.

<a id="cls:retained-roots"></a>
For a bit word $u=(u_0,\ldots,u_{n-1})\in\{0,1\}^n$, let $$\mathcal O_u=
  \{w\in\mathbb R^n:(2u_i-1)w_i\geq0\text{ for }0\leq i<n\}$$ be the associated closed orthant. A vector with a zero coordinate belongs to both choices of sign in that coordinate. For $j\geq1$, put $$R_*(n,d,j)=
  \left\{r\in R(n,d):
    \#\left\{u\in\{0,1\}^n:
       \#(W_n(r;d)\cap\mathcal O_u)\geq j(2r)^n
    \right\}\geq\frac{2^n}{d}\right\}.
  \label{cls:retained-roots}$$ All comparisons in this definition involve algebraic numbers and finite sets. In particular, $R(n,d)$ and $R_*(n,d,j)$ can be obtained by finite enumeration of integer polynomials and exact algebraic comparisons.

<a id="stmt-cls-classification"></a>
<div id="cls:classification" class="theorem">

<a id="cls:criterion"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Arithmetic criterion). *Define the parameter set $$\mathcal C=(0,1/2)\ \cup\
  \left[(1/2,1)\cap
  \bigcup_{d\geq2}\ \bigcap_{j\geq1}\
  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
  \bigcup_{r\in R_*(n,d,j)}[r-4^{-n},r)\right].
  \label{cls:criterion}$$ For every $\lambda\in(0,1)$, the measure $\nu_\lambda$ is singular if and only if $\lambda\in\mathcal C$, and is absolutely continuous if and only if $\lambda\notin\mathcal C$. Replacing $\limsup$ by $\liminf$ in <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a> gives the same set.*

</div>

Thus the degree multiplier $d$ may be fixed before the crowding level $j$ is chosen. For a singular parameter in $(1/2,1)$, each level $j$ is attained at every sufficiently large $n$, with the threshold in $n$ allowed to depend on $j$. The predicate uses only polynomial arithmetic, one-sided approximation, and counts of coefficient vectors. Determining membership for a particular parameter can nevertheless be difficult; the theorem does not supply a finite decision procedure for arbitrary real inputs.

<a id="cls:subsection-preliminaries"></a>
#### Measure-theoretic preliminaries

<a id="cls:prefix-sum"></a>
We use the bit form $$X_\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^\infty u_i\lambda^i,
  \qquad \mathbb P(u_i=0)=\mathbb P(u_i=1)=\tfrac12,$$ with law $\mu_\lambda$, and write $L=(1-\lambda)^{-1}$. The affine identity $Y_\lambda=2X_\lambda-L$ shows that $\mu_\lambda$ and $\nu_\lambda$ have the same measure type. For a word $u\in\{0,1\}^n$, put $$s_u(\lambda)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}u_i\lambda^i.
  \label{cls:prefix-sum}$$ Conditioned on this prefix, $X_\lambda$ lies in $s_u(\lambda)+\lambda^n[0,L]$.

<a id="stmt-cls-pure-type"></a>
<div id="cls:pure-type" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Pure type and the baseline parameters). *For $0<\lambda<1$, the measure $\mu_\lambda$ is either singular or absolutely continuous. It is singular for $\lambda<1/2$ and uniform on $[0,2]$ for $\lambda=1/2$. Consequently, $\nu_{1/2}$ is uniform on $[-2,2]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-pure-type"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-pure-type</summary>

*Proof.* Let $T_i(x)=i+\lambda x$ for $i=0,1$, and let $\mathcal T\rho=\tfrac12(T_0)_*\rho+\tfrac12(T_1)_*\rho$. Iteration from any probability measure $\rho$ gives the law of $$\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}u_i\lambda^i+\lambda^n Z,$$ where $Z$ has law $\rho$ and is independent of the bits. Since $\lambda^nZ\to0$ in probability, these laws converge weakly to $\mu_\lambda$. Hence $\mu_\lambda$ is the unique invariant probability of $\mathcal T$.

The operator $\mathcal T$ preserves absolute continuity and singularity. By uniqueness of the Lebesgue decomposition, the absolutely continuous and singular parts of its invariant probability are separately invariant. If both were nonzero, normalizing them would give two distinct invariant probabilities, a contradiction.

For $\lambda<1/2$, the support is covered, for every $n$, by $2^n$ intervals of length $L\lambda^n$. Its Lebesgue measure is therefore zero. For $\lambda=1/2$, the usual fair binary expansion gives the uniform law on $[0,2]$. ◻

</details>

We shall also use the following elementary formulation of concentration. It isolates the measure-theoretic input needed to establish membership in <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a>.

<a id="stmt-cls-compact-concentration"></a>
<div id="cls:compact-concentration" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Compact concentration). *A Borel probability measure $\rho$ on $\mathbb R$ is singular if and only if, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a compact set $K$ with $$\operatorname{Leb}(K)<\varepsilon,
  \qquad \rho(K)>1-\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-compact-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-compact-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* If $\rho$ is singular, apply inner regularity to a Borel null set of full $\rho$-measure. Conversely, choose such compact sets $K_m$ with $\varepsilon=2^{-m}$. The set $\limsup_m K_m$ has Lebesgue measure zero, since $\sum_m\operatorname{Leb}(K_m)<\infty$, and has $\rho$-measure one, since every union $\bigcup_{m\geq M}K_m$ has $\rho$-measure one. ◻

</details>

<a id="cls:subsection-forward"></a>
#### Algebraic clusters imply singularity

<a id="cls:slab"></a>
Suppose $\lambda\in(1/2,1)$ satisfies the condition in <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a>, and fix a degree multiplier $d$ witnessing it. For each $j$, there are arbitrarily large $n$ and $r\in R_*(n,d,j)$ such that $$r-4^{-n}\leq\lambda<r.$$ If $w\in W_n(r;d)$, choose a polynomial $p_q$ giving this projection. Its root $q$ satisfies $|q-\lambda|\leq2\cdot4^{-n}$. On any fixed compact subinterval of $(0,1)$ containing $\lambda$ in its interior, $$|p_q'(t)|\leq\sum_{k\geq1}k t^{k-1}=\frac1{(1-t)^2}.$$ The coefficients of index at least $n$ contribute at most $L\lambda^n$ at $\lambda$. Thus there is a constant $B=B(\lambda)$, independent of $n,d,j,w$, such that for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\left|\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_i\lambda^i\right|\leq B\lambda^n.
  \label{cls:slab}$$ Here we used $4^{-n}=o(\lambda^n)$.

If $w\in\mathcal O_u\cap\{-1,0,1\}^n$, then $v=u-w$ belongs to $\{0,1\}^n$. Different $w$ give different $v$. By <a href="#cls:retained-roots" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:retained-roots">[cls:retained-roots]</a>, at least $2^n/d$ words $u$ consequently have at least $j(2r)^n\geq j(2\lambda)^n$ neighbors $v$ satisfying $$|s_u(\lambda)-s_v(\lambda)|\leq B\lambda^n.$$

<a id="cls:forward-concentration"></a>
Partition the line into half-open bins of length $\lambda^n$, and let $\mathcal I$ be the bins containing at least one of these words $u$, where a word occupies the bin containing its prefix sum. For each $J\in\mathcal I$, choose one such word. Its neighbors lie in the $B\lambda^n$-enlargement of $J$. Each prefix sum can belong to the enlargements of at most $N_B=2\lceil B\rceil+3$ bins. Counting word–bin incidences yields $$\#\mathcal I\,j(2\lambda)^n\leq N_B2^n,
  \qquad
  \#\mathcal I\leq\frac{N_B\lambda^{-n}}j.$$ Enlarge each bin $J=[a,a+\lambda^n)$ to $[a,a+(1+L)\lambda^n]$, and denote their union by $E_j$. The tail bound following <a href="#cls:prefix-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:prefix-sum">[cls:prefix-sum]</a> gives $$\operatorname{Leb}(E_j)\leq\frac{(1+L)N_B}{j},
  \qquad \mu_\lambda(E_j)\geq\frac1d.
  \label{cls:forward-concentration}$$ Absolute continuity of a finite measure makes its mass uniformly small on sets of sufficiently small Lebesgue measure. Therefore <a href="#cls:forward-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:forward-concentration">[cls:forward-concentration]</a>, as $j\to\infty$, rules out absolute continuity. Lemma <a href="#cls:pure-type" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:pure-type">2</a> proves singularity.

<a id="cls:section-converse"></a>
### Constructing the algebraic approximants

We now prove the converse in Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a>. Throughout this section, $\lambda\in(1/2,1)$ is fixed, and we abbreviate $\mu=\mu_\lambda$, $X=X_\lambda$, and $s_u=s_u(\lambda)$. The first step is to turn concentration of $\mu$ into many close prefix sums with opposite first bits. Opposite first bits ensure that their difference polynomial has constant coefficient $-1$, as required by the algebraic criterion.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Overlap of the first-bit measures

Define subprobability measures $$\mu_i(A)=\mathbb P(X\in A,\ u_0=i),\qquad i=0,1.$$ Their sum is $\mu$. The following fact uses only $\lambda>1/2$, not singularity.

<a id="stmt-cls-first-bit-overlap"></a>
<div id="cls:first-bit-overlap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *There is a finite measure $\xi\ne0$ with $\xi\leq\mu_0$ and $\xi\leq\mu_1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-first-bit-overlap"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-first-bit-overlap</summary>

*Proof.* If no such measure exists, the densities of $\mu_0$ and $\mu_1$ with respect to $\mu$ have disjoint supports almost everywhere. Thus $\mu_0$ and $\mu_1$ are mutually singular. We will show that this makes the conditional entropy of the first $n$ bits, given a $\lambda^n$-quantization of $X$, arbitrarily small compared with $n$. There are only $O(\lambda^{-n})$ quantization cells, too few to carry the full entropy $n\log2$ of those bits. For every $\eta\in(0,1/2)$, inner regularity then gives disjoint compact sets $K_0,K_1$ such that $$\mathbb P(X\in K_{u_0})>1-\eta.$$ Let $\delta=\operatorname{dist}(K_0,K_1)>0$, and choose an integer $h\geq1$ with $\lambda^h<\delta/3$.

Set $Q_n=\lfloor X/\lambda^n\rfloor$. Given $Q_n$ and the bits $u_0,\ldots,u_{i-1}$, one can approximate $$X^{(i)}=\frac{X-\sum_{k<i}u_k\lambda^k}{\lambda^i}$$ to within $\lambda^{n-i}$. The pair $(X^{(i)},u_i)$ has the same law as $(X,u_0)$. For $i\leq n-h$, the approximation therefore determines an estimator of $u_i$ with error probability at most $\eta$: when $X^{(i)}\in K_{u_i}$, choose the nearer of the two compact sets.

Write $H$ for Shannon entropy using natural logarithms, and put $h_2(\eta)=-\eta\log\eta-(1-\eta)\log(1-\eta)$. The error-indicator argument gives $$H(u_i\mid Q_n,u_0,\ldots,u_{i-1})\leq h_2(\eta)
  \qquad(i\leq n-h).$$ Indeed, the estimator and its binary error indicator recover $u_i$; the entropy of that indicator is at most $h_2(\eta)$. The remaining at most $h$ bits have conditional entropy at most $h\log2$. Since $X\in[0,L]$, the variable $Q_n$ takes at most $L\lambda^{-n}+2$ values. The chain rule now gives $$n\log2
  \leq H(Q_n)+H(u_0,\ldots,u_{n-1}\mid Q_n)
  \leq n\log(1/\lambda)+n h_2(\eta)+O_{\lambda,\eta}(1).$$ Choose $\eta$ so small that $h_2(\eta)<\log(2\lambda)$ and let $n\to\infty$. This is a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cls-cross-pairs"></a>
<div id="cls:cross-pairs" class="proposition">

<a id="cls:cross-pair-bound"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Many close prefixes with opposite first bits). *If $\mu_\lambda$ is singular, there are constants $a\in(0,L/2)$ and $c>0$, depending only on $\lambda$, with the following property. For every $Q>1$ and all sufficiently large $n$, at least $c2^n$ words $u\in\{0,1\}^n$ with $u_0=0$ each have at least $Q(2\lambda)^n$ distinct neighbors $v\in\{0,1\}^n$ such that $$v_0=1,
  \qquad |s_u-s_v|\leq(L-a)\lambda^n.
  \label{cls:cross-pair-bound}$$ The constants $a,c$ are independent of $Q$ and $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-cross-pairs"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-cross-pairs</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $\xi$ from Lemma <a href="#cls:first-bit-overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:first-bit-overlap">4</a>, and write $b=\xi(\mathbb R)>0$. The endpoints $0,L$ have $\mu$-measure zero, because each requires all the bits to have one prescribed value. Choose $a\in(0,L/2)$ so that $\mu([a,L-a])>1-b/8$.

For each $n$, let $X^{(n)}=\sum_{k\geq0}u_{n+k}\lambda^k$, and restrict the first-bit measures by defining $$\mu'_{i,n}(A)=
  \mathbb P(X\in A,\ u_0=i,\ X^{(n)}\in[a,L-a]).$$ The sum of the masses discarded from $\mu_0,\mu_1$ is less than $b/8$, since $X^{(n)}$ has law $\mu$.

<a id="cls:common-bin-mass"></a>
Fix $Q>1$. By Lemma <a href="#cls:compact-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:compact-concentration">3</a>, choose a compact set $K$ with $\operatorname{Leb}(K)<\varepsilon$ and $\mu(K)>1-\varepsilon$, where $$\varepsilon<b/8,
  \qquad \frac{2\varepsilon Q}{a}<\frac b4.$$ Then $\xi(K)>7b/8$. Partition the line into half-open intervals of length $a\lambda^n$, and let $\mathcal J_n$ consist of those meeting $K$. Compactness of $K$ implies, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\#\mathcal J_n\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{a\lambda^n}.$$ To see this, their union is contained in the closed $a\lambda^n$-neighborhood of $K$, whose Lebesgue measure tends to $\operatorname{Leb}(K)$. Moreover, $$\sum_{J\in\mathcal J_n}
       \min_{i=0,1}\mu'_{i,n}(J)\geq\frac{3b}{4}.
  \label{cls:common-bin-mass}$$ Indeed, the corresponding sum with $\mu_i$ in place of $\mu'_{i,n}$ is at least $\xi(K)$, and restricting the two measures loses less than $b/8$ in total.

Let $U_i(J)$ be the set of words $u$ of length $n$ with $u_0=i$ for which $$\bigl(s_u+\lambda^n[a,L-a]\bigr)\cap J\ne\varnothing.$$ Each prefix has probability $2^{-n}$, so $\mu'_{i,n}(J)\leq2^{-n}\#U_i(J)$. Discard the intervals for which $\#U_1(J)<Q(2\lambda)^n$. Their contribution to <a href="#cls:common-bin-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:common-bin-mass">[cls:common-bin-mass]</a> is at most $$\frac{2\varepsilon}{a\lambda^n}\,
  2^{-n}Q(2\lambda)^n
  =\frac{2\varepsilon Q}{a}<\frac b4.$$ Thus the sum of the minima over the remaining intervals is at least $b/2$.

A fixed interval $s_u+\lambda^n[a,L-a]$ meets at most $$C_a=\left\lceil\frac{L-2a}{a}\right\rceil+2$$ of the bins. If $\mathcal U$ is the union of $U_0(J)$ over the retained bins, it follows that $$\frac b2\leq2^{-n}\sum_{J\text{ retained}}\#U_0(J)
       \leq2^{-n}C_a\#\mathcal U.$$ Take $c=b/(2C_a)$. For every $u\in\mathcal U$, choose one retained bin $J$ with $u\in U_0(J)$. All $v\in U_1(J)$ satisfy <a href="#cls:cross-pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:cross-pair-bound">[cls:cross-pair-bound]</a>: two points in $J$ differ by less than $a\lambda^n$, and two tails in $\lambda^n[a,L-a]$ differ by at most $(L-2a)\lambda^n$. There are at least $Q(2\lambda)^n$ such neighbors. ◻

</details>

<a id="cls:subsection-completion"></a>
#### Completion to irreducible polynomials

For every pair in Proposition <a href="#cls:cross-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:cross-pairs">5</a>, the difference $w=u-v$ has $w_0=-1$ and $|\sum_{i<n}w_i\lambda^i|\leq(L-a)\lambda^n$. We next extend any such coefficient vector to a polynomial whose root lies in $(\lambda,\lambda+4^{-n}/2]$. The first lemma ensures that a bounded-coefficient polynomial cannot remain too flat throughout this short interval.

<a id="stmt-cls-finite-order"></a>
<div id="cls:finite-order" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Uniform finite-order nonvanishing). *Fix $\lambda\in(0,1)$. There are an integer $M\geq1$ and constants $c_0,x_0>0$ such that every real polynomial $p(t)=-1+\sum_{k\geq1}a_kt^k$ with $|a_k|\leq1$ satisfies $$\sup_{t\in[\lambda,\lambda+x]}|p(t)|\geq c_0x^M
  \qquad(0<x<x_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-finite-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-finite-order</summary>

<a id="cls:jet-bound"></a>
*Proof.* There exist $M\geq1$ and $\delta>0$ such that $$\max_{0\leq k\leq M}
     \left|\frac{p^{(k)}(\lambda)}{k!}\right|\geq\delta
  \label{cls:jet-bound}$$ for every polynomial in the stated class. Otherwise, for each $m$ one could choose a polynomial for which all these quantities through order $m$ are less than $1/m$. Extend its coefficient sequence by zeros. A coefficientwise convergent subsequence exists by compactness of $[-1,1]^{\mathbb N}$. The associated series converge, with every derivative, uniformly on compact subsets of the unit disk. Their limit would have all derivatives zero at $\lambda$ and constant coefficient $-1$, contradicting the identity theorem.

For fixed $M$, equivalence of norms on polynomials of degree at most $M$ gives $\kappa_M>0$ such that $$\sup_{0\leq t\leq1}\left|\sum_{k=0}^M b_kt^k\right|
       \geq\kappa_M\max_{k\leq M}|b_k|.$$ Apply this to the Taylor polynomial of $p(\lambda+xt)$. For $x\leq1$, <a href="#cls:jet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:jet-bound">[cls:jet-bound]</a> makes the right-hand side at least $\kappa_M\delta x^M$. The coefficient bound gives a uniform bound on the $(M+1)$st derivative in a fixed neighborhood of $\lambda$, so the Taylor remainder is $O_\lambda(x^{M+1})$, with $M$ fixed. Reducing $x_0$ proves the assertion with $c_0=\kappa_M\delta/2$. ◻

</details>

To impose irreducibility without disturbing the prescribed coefficients, we use the prime-polynomial theorem in arithmetic progressions. We include the deduction needed here from the function-field Riemann hypothesis; its character-sum formulation is recalled in (Gorodetsky and Kovaleva 2024, Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GorodetskyKovaleva2024](#ref-GorodetskyKovaleva2024)].

<a id="stmt-cls-prescribed-prime"></a>
<div id="cls:prescribed-prime" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Irreducibles with prescribed initial coefficients). *Fix integers $D\geq2$ and $d>2D$. For all sufficiently large $n$, every polynomial $F\in\mathbb F_2[t]$ with $\deg F<Dn$ and $F(0)=1$ has a monic irreducible completion $P\in\mathbb F_2[t]$ satisfying $$\deg P=dn,
  \qquad P\equiv F+t^{Dn}\pmod{t^{Dn+1}}.$$ The threshold in $n$ is uniform in $F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-prescribed-prime"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-prescribed-prime</summary>

<a id="cls:prime-progression-bound"></a>
*Proof.* For a monic polynomial $f$, write $\Lambda(f)=\deg P$ if $f=P^k$ for a monic irreducible $P$, and $\Lambda(f)=0$ otherwise. Let $\chi$ be a nonprincipal Dirichlet character modulo $t^\ell$, extended by zero to nonunits. Its $L$-function is a polynomial of degree at most $\ell-1$, with inverse roots $\alpha_j$ satisfying $|\alpha_j|\leq\sqrt2$; this bound includes trivial factors. Taking the logarithmic derivative of the Euler product gives $$\left|\sum_{\substack{f\text{ monic}\\\deg f=s}}
       \Lambda(f)\chi(f)\right|
  =\left|\sum_j\alpha_j^s\right|
  \leq\ell2^{s/2}.$$ For the principal character, the same sum is $2^s-1$, by the zeta function of $\mathbb F_2[t]$ after removal of the prime $t$. Character orthogonality therefore gives, in every invertible residue class $A$ modulo $t^\ell$, $$\sum_{\substack{f\text{ monic},\ \deg f=s\\f\equiv A\bmod t^\ell}}
       \Lambda(f)
  \geq \frac{2^s-1}{2^{\ell-1}}-\ell2^{s/2}.
  \label{cls:prime-progression-bound}$$ The contribution from proper prime powers, even without the congruence restriction, is at most $$\sum_{\substack{k\mid s\\k\geq2}}2^{s/k}
       \leq s2^{s/2}.$$ Take $\ell=Dn+1$, $s=dn$, and $A=F+t^{Dn}$. This is an invertible class. The main term in <a href="#cls:prime-progression-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:prime-progression-bound">[cls:prime-progression-bound]</a> has order $2^{(d-D)n}$, whereas the character error and proper-power contribution are $O(n2^{dn/2})$. Since $d>2D$, at least one irreducible polynomial remains for all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly in $A$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cls-realization"></a>
<div id="cls:realization" class="proposition">

<a id="cls:realization-hypothesis"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Realization of a polynomial prefix). *Fix $\lambda\in(1/2,1)$ and $a\in(0,L)$, where $L=(1-\lambda)^{-1}$. There is an integer $d_0$ such that, for each integer $d\geq d_0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, the following assertion holds uniformly over $w\in\{-1,0,1\}^n$. If $$w_0=-1,
  \qquad
  \left|\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}w_i\lambda^i\right|
      \leq(L-a)\lambda^n,
  \label{cls:realization-hypothesis}$$ then some $r\in R(n,d)$ satisfies $$\lambda<r\leq\lambda+\tfrac12 4^{-n},
  \qquad \pi_n(p_r)=w.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-realization"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-realization</summary>

*Proof.* We first append digits to make the polynomial small at $\lambda$. We then construct two irreducible completions with opposite signs there but the same sign at a nearby point to its right. Uniform finite-order nonvanishing supplies that second point, and the intermediate value theorem supplies a root between the two points.

<a id="cls:D-choice"></a>
Let $M,c_0,x_0$ be supplied by Lemma <a href="#cls:finite-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:finite-order">6</a>. Choose an integer $D\geq2$ with $$\lambda^D<4^{-M},
  \label{cls:D-choice}$$ and then take $d_0=2D+1$.

<a id="cls:residual"></a>
Start with the polynomial in <a href="#cls:realization-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:realization-hypothesis">[cls:realization-hypothesis]</a> and append coefficients through index $Dn-1$. If the next index is $k$, let $e$ be the negative of the current value at $\lambda$, divided by $\lambda^k$. Appending $e_0\in\{-1,0,1\}$ changes this residual to $$e\longmapsto\frac{e-e_0}{\lambda}.$$ Choose a nearest digit $e_0$. Once $|e|\leq1$, the next residual has absolute value at most $1/(2\lambda)<1$, so this bound is preserved. Before that time, the residual keeps its sign and its absolute value updates by $$|e|\longmapsto\frac{|e|-1}{\lambda}
        =L+\frac{|e|-L}{\lambda}.$$ The initial bound $|e|\leq L-a$ implies that $|e|\leq1$ is reached within a number of steps depending only on $\lambda,a$. Indeed, after $k$ steps without reaching this interval the absolute value is at most $L-a\lambda^{-k}$, which eventually falls below $1$. Consequently, for all sufficiently large $n$, the polynomial $p_0$ constructed through index $Dn-1$ satisfies $$|p_0(\lambda)|\leq\frac{\lambda^{Dn}}{2\lambda}.
  \label{cls:residual}$$

Put $x_n=4^{-n}/2$. By Lemma <a href="#cls:finite-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:finite-order">6</a>, there is $t_n\in[\lambda,\lambda+x_n]$ with $|p_0(t_n)|\geq c_0x_n^M$. Meanwhile every possible appendage with coefficients of absolute value at most one, starting at index $Dn$, is bounded throughout this interval by $$\frac{(\lambda+x_n)^{Dn}}{1-\lambda-x_n}=o(x_n^M).$$ This follows from <a href="#cls:D-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:D-choice">[cls:D-choice]</a>, since $Dn x_n\to0$. The estimate is uniform in the prefix and in the eventual length of the appendage.

Fix $d\geq d_0$. Apply Lemma <a href="#cls:prescribed-prime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:prescribed-prime">7</a> to the reduction of $p_0$ modulo $2$, obtaining a monic irreducible polynomial $P$ of degree $dn$ with $P\equiv p_0+t^{Dn}\pmod{t^{Dn+1}}$. Represent its coefficients of indices $Dn,\ldots,dn$ by integers $b_k\in\{0,1\}$, and let $$T(t)=\sum_{k=Dn}^{dn}b_kt^k,
  \qquad p_\pm(t)=p_0(t)\pm T(t).$$ Here $b_{Dn}=b_{dn}=1$. Thus $T(\lambda)\geq\lambda^{Dn}$, and <a href="#cls:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:residual">[cls:residual]</a> implies $p_-(\lambda)<0<p_+(\lambda)$. At $t_n$, the negligible-tail estimate shows that both polynomials have the same nonzero sign as $p_0(t_n)$. One of them therefore has a real root $$r\in(\lambda,t_n]\subset(\lambda,\lambda+x_n].$$

Both $p_\pm$ reduce to $P$ modulo $2$, so they are irreducible over $\mathbb Q$. They have degree $dn$, leading coefficient $\pm1$, constant coefficient $-1$, and all coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$. Their roots are algebraic units. For sufficiently large $n$, the chosen $r$ is in $(1/2,1)$, belongs to $R(n,d)$, and its signed minimal polynomial is the chosen $p_\pm$. Its first $n$ coefficients are $w$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Proof of the converse and the quantifiers

Suppose $\mu_\lambda$ is singular. Proposition <a href="#cls:cross-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:cross-pairs">5</a> gives constants $a,c>0$. Choose one integer $d$ large enough for Proposition <a href="#cls:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:realization">8</a> and also satisfying $1/d<c$. This choice depends on $\lambda$ alone.

Fix any integer $j\geq1$ and choose $Q>2j$. For all sufficiently large $n$, each of at least $c2^n$ words $u$ has at least $Q(2\lambda)^n$ neighbors $v$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#cls:cross-pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:cross-pairs">5</a>. For each difference $w=u-v$, Proposition <a href="#cls:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:realization">8</a> supplies a root $r_w\in R(n,d)$ with signed minimal polynomial projecting to $w$, and $$\lambda<r_w\leq\lambda+\tfrac12 4^{-n}.$$ Choose any one of these roots as a center $r$. All of them lie within $4^{-n}$ of $r$, so all their projected vectors belong to $W_n(r;d)$. For fixed $u$, its different neighbors give distinct vectors $u-v$, each in $\mathcal O_u$. Furthermore, $$1\leq\left(\frac r\lambda\right)^n
       \leq\left(1+\frac{4^{-n}}{2\lambda}\right)^n\longrightarrow1$$ uniformly in the choice of center. Hence, for all sufficiently large $n$, each of these $c2^n$ orthants contains at least $j(2r)^n$ distinct vectors of $W_n(r;d)$. Since $c>1/d$, we have $r\in R_*(n,d,j)$ and $\lambda\in[r-4^{-n},r)$.

We have proved that a single $d$ works for every $j$, at every sufficiently large $n$. This proves membership in the expression <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a> with $\liminf$. Membership in that expression implies membership with $\limsup$, which implies singularity by Section <a href="#cls:subsection-forward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:subsection-forward">2.2</a>. Lemma <a href="#cls:pure-type" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:pure-type">2</a> handles $\lambda\leq1/2$ and supplies the absolute continuity of every remaining parameter. This completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a>.

<a id="cls:section-classical"></a>
### Recovery of classical parameter classes

The arithmetic criterion is compatible with the classical norm separation argument for Garsia parameters and the Fourier argument for Pisot parameters. The first example directly bounds the crowding in <a href="#cls:retained-roots" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:retained-roots">[cls:retained-roots]</a>; the second establishes singularity and then uses Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a> to obtain the approximation property.

<a id="stmt-cls-garsia"></a>
<div id="cls:garsia" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (Garsia parameters, (Garsia 1962) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Garsia1962](#ref-Garsia1962)]). *Let $\beta\in(1,2)$ be an algebraic integer of absolute norm $2$ whose conjugates all have modulus greater than $1$. Then $1/\beta\notin
\mathcal C$, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is absolutely continuous.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-garsia"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-garsia</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\lambda=1/\beta$. Consider distinct vectors $w,w'\in\{-1,0,1\}^n$ lying in one closed orthant. Their difference has coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$, so $$P(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(w_i-w'_i)t^{n-1-i}$$ is a nonzero polynomial of that coefficient type. It cannot vanish at $\beta$. Otherwise, after removing any power of $t$ dividing $P$, its constant coefficient would be $\pm1$, while divisibility by the monic minimal polynomial of $\beta$ would force that coefficient to be divisible by $2$.

<a id="cls:garsia-spacing"></a>
For every other conjugate $\gamma$ of $\beta$, $$|P(\gamma)|\leq\frac{|\gamma|^n}{|\gamma|-1}.$$ The nonzero algebraic integer $P(\beta)$ has absolute norm at least one. Since the product of the moduli of all conjugates of $\beta$ is $2$, the preceding estimate gives a constant $c_\beta>0$ such that $$\left|\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(w_i-w'_i)\lambda^i\right|
  =\beta^{-(n-1)}|P(\beta)|\geq c_\beta2^{-n}.
  \label{cls:garsia-spacing}$$

For any potential hit of <a href="#cls:criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:criterion">[cls:criterion]</a> at a sufficiently large $n$, all projected vectors satisfy the slab bound <a href="#cls:slab" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:slab">[cls:slab]</a>. Within any one orthant, <a href="#cls:garsia-spacing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cls:garsia-spacing">[cls:garsia-spacing]</a> bounds their number by $$1+\frac{2B\lambda^n}{c_\beta2^{-n}}
       \leq C_\beta(2\lambda)^n.$$ Choose an integer $j>C_\beta$. Since a hit has $r>\lambda$, no orthant can then meet the threshold $j(2r)^n$. This excludes all sufficiently large $n$, for every degree multiplier $d$. Thus $\lambda\notin\mathcal C$, and Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a> gives absolute continuity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cls-pisot"></a>
<div id="cls:pisot" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10** (Reciprocal Pisot parameters, (Erdős 1939) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Erdos1939](#ref-Erdos1939)]). *If $\beta\in(1,2)$ is a Pisot number, then $1/\beta\in\mathcal C$, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cls-pisot"><summary>Proof of stmt-cls-pisot</summary>

*Proof.* A Pisot number is an algebraic integer greater than one whose other conjugates have modulus less than one. Here its nonzero integer norm has absolute value less than $\beta<2$, so $\beta$ is a unit. Consequently $\beta^\ell$ is an algebraic integer for every $\ell\in\mathbb Z$, and it can never be a half-integer of odd numerator. In particular, all factors $\cos(\pi\beta^\ell)$ are nonzero.

For negative $\ell$, the quantity $\beta^\ell$ tends exponentially to zero. For positive $\ell$, its distance to an integer tends exponentially to zero: the trace of $\beta^\ell$ is an integer and the contributions of the other conjugates decay exponentially. The quadratic behavior of $-\log|\cos(\pi t)|$ near the integers therefore gives $$\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z}-\log|\cos(\pi\beta^\ell)|<\infty.$$ With $\lambda=1/\beta$, independence of the signs yields $$\left|\mathbb E\exp(i\pi\beta^nY_\lambda)\right|
     =\prod_{\ell\leq n}|\cos(\pi\beta^\ell)|
     \geq\prod_{\ell\in\mathbb Z}|\cos(\pi\beta^\ell)|>0.$$ The Riemann–Lebesgue lemma rules out absolute continuity. Lemma <a href="#cls:pure-type" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:pure-type">2</a> gives singularity, and Theorem <a href="#cls:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cls:classification">1</a> gives $\lambda\in\mathcal C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sal:section"></a>
### Degree-four Salem parameters

For a degree-four Salem number $\beta$, the two conjugates on the unit circle form a single complex-conjugate pair. We use simultaneous control of this pair to construct many Fourier phases with appreciable expectation. After removing integer trace terms, widely separated phases depend, up to a small error, on disjoint blocks of the Bernoulli signs. Their averages therefore distinguish the Bernoulli convolution from Lebesgue measure.

<a id="stmt-sal-main"></a>
<div id="sal:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 11**. *Let $\beta\in(1,2)$ be a Salem number of degree four. Then the unbiased Bernoulli convolution $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.*

</div>

The proof has two quantitative requirements. The Fourier products must lose little mass before the algebraic trace becomes an accurate integer approximation; the remaining trace errors must then stay small over many disjoint blocks. The multiplier construction below supplies both requirements. The trace approximation is the familiar arithmetic ingredient in the Fourier analysis of Pisot and Salem parameters (Erdős 1939) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Erdos1939](#ref-Erdos1939)]; see in particular Salem’s small-conjugate construction (Salem 1963, chap. III, Section 3, Theorem III, pp. 28–29) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Salem1963](#ref-Salem1963)]. The estimates here also control the losses accumulated while the multiplier changes.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Multipliers with small unit-circle conjugates

Fix $\beta$ as in Theorem <a href="#sal:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:main">11</a>, and write its conjugates as $\beta^{-1},z,\bar z$, where $z=e^{i\theta}$. The number $\beta$ is a unit: the product of its four conjugates is $1$. Moreover, $\theta/\pi$ is irrational, since otherwise $z$, and hence its conjugate $\beta$, would be a root of unity.

<a id="sal:multipliers"></a>
<a id="sal:trace"></a>
For positive integers $k_1,k_2,\ldots$, to be chosen, define $$\label{sal:multipliers}
\begin{aligned}
 A_m&=\prod_{h=1}^m(\beta^{k_h}-\beta^{-k_h}),
 &b_m&=\sum_{h=1}^m k_h,\\
 c_m&=\prod_{h=1}^m(z^{k_h}-z^{-k_h}),
 &\delta_m&=|c_m|,\qquad L_m=\log(1/\delta_m).
\end{aligned}$$ Here and throughout this section logarithms are natural. Irrationality ensures $\delta_m>0$, and $0<A_m\le\beta^{b_m}$. For $j\in\mathbb Z$, put $u_{m,j}=A_m\beta^j$. All these numbers are algebraic integers, because $\beta$ is a unit. Their field traces are the integers $$\label{sal:trace}
 T_{m,j}=u_{m,j}+(-1)^m u_{m,-j}
                  +2\operatorname{Re}(c_mz^j)\in\mathbb Z.$$ Indeed, the embedding taking $\beta$ to $\beta^{-1}$ takes $A_m$ to $(-1)^mA_m$. Also $u_{m,j}\notin\mathbb Z+\tfrac12$, since a rational algebraic integer is an integer. Thus none of the cosine factors used below vanishes.

The construction below balances the decrease of $\delta_m$ against the cosine losses generated by $A_m$. Eventually $L_m$ grows by a factor greater than two while $k_{m+1}\delta_m^2$ tends to zero; these are the margins used in the loss estimate below. It also keeps $b_m$ small compared with $\delta_m^{-1}$, leaving room for many separated blocks of signs. A uniform lower bound on the cosines will permit a quadratic estimate for perturbations of their logarithms.

<a id="stmt-sal-construction"></a>
<div id="sal:construction" class="lemma">

<a id="sal:growth-initial"></a>
<a id="sal:growth"></a>
<a id="sal:cosine-floor"></a>
<a id="sal:small-errors"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *There are positive integers $k_m$ and a constant $\gamma>0$ for which the numbers $L_m$ in <a href="#sal:multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:multipliers">[sal:multipliers]</a> are positive and satisfy the following properties. Set $$D=\frac{10}{\log\beta},\qquad
 K_m=\left\lceil2b_m+DL_m\right\rceil\quad(m\ge2).$$ Then $A_m\ge1$ for every $m$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 K_m\le k_{m+1}\le e^{1.9L_m},\qquad
 L_{m+1}&\ge1.75L_m &&(m\ge2),\label{sal:growth-initial}\\
 \log K_m\le .75L_m,\qquad
 L_{m+1}&\ge2.1L_m &&(m\ge3).\label{sal:growth} 
\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\label{sal:cosine-floor}
 |\cos(\pi u_{m,j})|\ge\gamma
       \quad(m\ge1,\ j\in\mathbb Z),$$ and, for $m\ge2$, $$\label{sal:small-errors}
 2\delta_m\le\frac{\gamma}{2\pi},\qquad
 \beta^{b_m-k_{m+1}}\le e^{-10L_m}
                       \le\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sal-construction"><summary>Proof of stmt-sal-construction</summary>

<a id="sal:dirichlet"></a>
*Proof.* Write $\|x\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}$ for distance to the nearest integer. The pigeonhole principle gives, for every integer $N\ge1$, an integer $q\in[1,N]$ such that $$\label{sal:dirichlet}
 \|q\theta/\pi\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}\le N^{-1}.$$ For example, place the $N+1$ fractional parts of $0,\theta/\pi,\ldots,N\theta/\pi$ in $N$ intervals of length $1/N$. Since $\theta/\pi$ is irrational, the possible $q$ in <a href="#sal:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:dirichlet">[sal:dirichlet]</a> are unbounded as $N\to\infty$. In particular there are arbitrarily large $q$ with $\|q\theta/\pi\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}\le1/q$.

Choose and fix $k_1$ so that $A_1>1$ and $\delta_1<1/16$. Define $$\gamma_m=\inf_{j\in\mathbb Z}|\cos(\pi u_{m,j})|.$$ At this point $\gamma_1>0$: as $j\to-\infty$, $u_{1,j}\to0$; as $j\to+\infty$, the trace identity shows that the absolute cosine differs from $|\cos(\pi u_{1,-j})|$ by at most $2\pi\delta_1<\pi/8$. The remaining finite set of factors contains no zero.

Next choose $k_2>k_1$ from the unbounded sequence satisfying $\|k_2\theta/\pi\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}\le1/k_2$. We will impose finitely many lower thresholds on this choice. Since $$\delta_2\le\frac{2\pi\delta_1}{k_2},\qquad
 L_2\ge\log k_2-\log(2\pi\delta_1),$$ both $k_2$ and $L_2$ can be made arbitrarily large. In particular, $\log K_2\le1.1L_2$ for every sufficiently large such choice: the last inequality bounds $k_2$ by a fixed multiple of $e^{L_2}$, whereas the other contribution $DL_2$ to $K_2$ is only linear in $L_2$.

For $m\ge2$, suppose $L=L_m$ is large and $\log K_m\le1.1L$. Take $N=\lfloor e^{1.9L}\rfloor$ and choose $q$ by <a href="#sal:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:dirichlet">[sal:dirichlet]</a>. Define <a id="sal:choice-k"></a>$$\label{sal:choice-k}
 k_{m+1}=\begin{cases}
 q,&q\ge K_m,\\
 \lceil K_m/q\rceil q,&q<K_m.
 \end{cases}$$ Let $L'=L_{m+1}$. If $q\ge K_m$, then $$\delta_{m+1}\le2\pi\delta_m/N,\qquad
 L'\ge2.9L-O(1)\ge2.8L,
 \qquad b_{m+1}\le2e^{1.9L}.$$ If $q<K_m$, the multiplier $\lceil K_m/q\rceil$ is at most $2K_m$ and $K_m\le k_{m+1}<2K_m$. Using $|\sin(\pi x)|\le\pi\|x\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}$ gives $$\delta_{m+1}\le4\pi\delta_mK_m/N,
 \qquad L'\ge2.9L-\log K_m-O(1)\ge1.75L,$$ and $b_{m+1}\le3e^{1.1L}$. In both cases $K_m\le k_{m+1}\le e^{1.9L}$ when $L$ is sufficiently large.

These estimates also give $\log K_{m+1}\le .75L'$. To see this without an upper bound on $L'$, write $$K_{m+1}\le2b_{m+1}+DL'+1.$$ The bound on $b_{m+1}$ makes the first term at most $\tfrac12e^{.75L'}$ for large $L$, since $1.9<.75\cdot2.8$ in the first case and $1.1<.75\cdot1.75$ in the second. The remaining term satisfies the same bound for every sufficiently large $L'$. Thus the construction iterates. From $m=3$ onward, the improved bound $\log K_m\le .75L_m$ gives $L_{m+1}\ge(2.9-.75)L_m-O(1)\ge2.1L_m$ in the second case; the first case already gives more. This proves <a href="#sal:growth-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:growth-initial">[sal:growth-initial]</a>–<a href="#sal:growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:growth">[sal:growth]</a> after a lower threshold on $L_2$ depending only on $\beta$.

<a id="sal:shift"></a>
<a id="sal:floor-recurrence"></a>
It remains to choose $k_2$ large enough to obtain the cosine floor. For $j\le0$ and $k=k_{m+1}$, the exact identity $$\label{sal:shift}
 u_{m+1,j}=u_{m,j+k}-u_{m,j-k},\qquad
 |u_{m,j-k}|\le\beta^{b_m-k}\beta^j$$ and the Lipschitz bound for the cosine give a lower bound $\gamma_m-\pi\beta^{b_m-k}$. For positive $j$, reflect to $-j$ using <a href="#sal:trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:trace">[sal:trace]</a> at level $m+1$. Consequently $$\label{sal:floor-recurrence}
 \gamma_{m+1}\ge\gamma_m
              -\pi\beta^{b_m-k_{m+1}}-2\pi\delta_{m+1}.$$ For $m\ge2$, the inequality $k_{m+1}\ge2b_m+DL_m$ implies $\beta^{b_m-k_{m+1}}\le e^{-10L_m}$. Also $L_m\ge1.75^{m-2}L_2$. Thus the sum of the decrements in <a href="#sal:floor-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:floor-recurrence">[sal:floor-recurrence]</a> is at most $$\pi\beta^{k_1-k_2}+2\pi e^{-L_2}
 +\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}
   \left(\pi e^{-10\cdot1.75^rL_2}
         +2\pi e^{-1.75^{r+1}L_2}\right).$$ This tends to zero as $k_2\to\infty$ along the chosen sequence. Choose $k_2$ so that this sum is less than $\gamma_1/10$, and so that $2e^{-L_2}$ and $e^{-10L_2}$ are at most $\gamma_1/(4\pi)$. These requirements are compatible with the earlier lower thresholds. Now fix $k_2$ and the resulting sequence, and put $\gamma=\gamma_1/2$. Equations <a href="#sal:cosine-floor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:cosine-floor">[sal:cosine-floor]</a>–<a href="#sal:small-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:small-errors">[sal:small-errors]</a> follow. Finally, $k_m$ is increasing and every factor $\beta^{k_m}-\beta^{-k_m}$ is at least its first factor $A_1>1$, so $A_m\ge1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The loss in the cosine products

Fix the sequence furnished by Lemma <a href="#sal:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:construction">12</a>. Constants denoted by $C$ below may change from line to line, but are independent of $m$ and of the phase index. Define $$f(u)=-\log|\cos(\pi u)|
       \quad\text{for }u\notin\mathbb Z+\tfrac12,
 \qquad S_m=\sum_{j\le0}f(u_{m,j}).$$ Each $S_m$ is finite: its terms have a geometrically decaying tail because $u_{m,j}\to0$ as $j\to-\infty$ and $f(u)=O(u^2)$ near zero. The next estimate controls the entire negative-power contribution even though $A_m$ becomes large.

<a id="stmt-sal-loss"></a>
<div id="sal:loss" class="lemma">

<a id="sal:total-loss"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *For the sequence in Lemma <a href="#sal:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:construction">12</a>, $S_m=o(L_m)$. Furthermore, with $\eta=.01$, for $m\ge2$ and $n\ge0$, $$\label{sal:total-loss}
 \sum_{j\le n}f(u_{m,j})
       \le(2+\eta)S_m+Cn\delta_m^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sal-loss"><summary>Proof of stmt-sal-loss</summary>

<a id="sal:taylor"></a>
*Proof.* We first record a perturbation estimate. If $|\cos(\pi u)|\ge\gamma$ and $|v|\le\gamma/(2\pi)$, then $$\label{sal:taylor}
 f(u+v)\le(1+\eta)f(u)+Cv^2.$$ Indeed, the absolute cosine is at least $\gamma/2$ along the segment from $u$ to $u+v$, so $f''$ is bounded there. Also $$|f'(u)|\le\frac{\pi}{\gamma}\sqrt{2f(u)},$$ because $1-|\cos(\pi u)|^2\le2f(u)$. Taylor’s formula and $|f'(u)v|\le\eta f(u)+Cv^2$ prove <a href="#sal:taylor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:taylor">[sal:taylor]</a>.

The function $f$ is even and $1$-periodic. Reflecting positive indices by <a href="#sal:trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:trace">[sal:trace]</a>, and using <a href="#sal:small-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:small-errors">[sal:small-errors]</a>, gives <a id="sal:reflection-loss"></a>$$\label{sal:reflection-loss}
