 f(u_{m,i})\le(1+\eta)f(u_{m,-i})+C\delta_m^2
                     \quad(m\ge2,\ i\ge1).$$ Summing this inequality proves <a href="#sal:total-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:total-loss">[sal:total-loss]</a>.

To estimate $S_{m+1}$, apply <a href="#sal:taylor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:taylor">[sal:taylor]</a> to <a href="#sal:shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:shift">[sal:shift]</a>, with base point $u_{m,j+k}$ and $v=-u_{m,j-k}$, where $k=k_{m+1}$. The cosine floor holds at every base point, including those with positive index. The squared perturbations have a geometric sum, so $$\begin{aligned}
 S_{m+1}
 &\le(1+\eta)\left(S_m+\sum_{i=1}^k f(u_{m,i})\right)
       +C\beta^{2(b_m-k)}\\
 &\le(1+\eta)(2+\eta)S_m+C(1+k\delta_m^2).
\end{aligned}$$ By <a href="#sal:growth-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:growth-initial">[sal:growth-initial]</a>, $k\delta_m^2\le e^{-.1L_m}\le1$. Therefore $S_{m+1}\le rS_m+C$, where $r=(1.01)(2.01)=2.0301$. The already fixed value $S_2$ is finite, so iteration gives $S_m=O(r^{m-2})$. In contrast, <a href="#sal:growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:growth">[sal:growth]</a> gives $L_m\ge2.1^{m-3}L_3$ for $m\ge3$. Since $r<2.1$, their ratio tends to zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Independent blocks of Fourier phases

<a id="sal:expectation"></a>
We now turn the product estimates into sets of almost full $\nu_{1/\beta}$-measure and vanishing Lebesgue measure. The comparison of phase averages under two measures is analogous to the separation argument for Riesz products in (Peyrière 1975, sec. 1.1, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Peyriere1975](#ref-Peyriere1975)]. Here the needed estimates follow from the independent signs, as we now show. Work on the probability space of independent uniform signs $(\epsilon_\ell)_{\ell\ge0}$, and write $$Y=\sum_{\ell\ge0}\epsilon_\ell\beta^{-\ell},\qquad
 W_{m,n}=\exp(i\pi A_m\beta^nY)\quad(n\ge0).$$ The law of $Y$ is $\nu_{1/\beta}$, supported on $I=[-(1-\beta^{-1})^{-1},(1-\beta^{-1})^{-1}]$. Independence, followed by passage to the limit in the convergent sign series, gives $$\label{sal:expectation}
 \mathbb EW_{m,n}=\prod_{j=-\infty}^n\cos(\pi u_{m,j}).$$ This is a nonzero real number; the negative-index tail has a convergent sum of logarithmic losses.

<a id="sal:block-count"></a>
<a id="sal:mean-lower"></a>
Set $$J_m=\lfloor e^{1.5L_m}\rfloor,\qquad
 H_m=\lceil b_m+DL_m\rceil,$$ $$\mathcal N_m=\{H_m+s(2H_m+1):s\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}\}\cap[0,J_m],$$ and let $M_m=|\mathcal N_m|$. All subsequent estimates concern sufficiently large $m$. Since $H_m\le K_m\le e^{.75L_m}$, $$\label{sal:block-count}
 e^{.7L_m}\le M_m\le J_m+1.$$ Lemma <a href="#sal:loss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:loss">13</a> and $J_m\delta_m^2\le e^{-.5L_m}$ show that $$\label{sal:mean-lower}
 |\mathbb EW_{m,n}|\ge e^{-L_m/20}
             \quad(0\le n\le J_m).$$

<a id="sal:window-phase"></a>
For $n\in\mathcal N_m$, retain only the signs whose indices lie in $[n-H_m,n+H_m]$, and define $$\label{sal:window-phase}
 \widetilde W_{m,n}
 =(-1)^{\sum_{j=H_m+1}^nT_{m,j}}
   \exp\left(i\pi\sum_{\ell=n-H_m}^{n+H_m}
                      u_{m,n-\ell}\epsilon_\ell\right).$$ The integer sum in the prefactor is empty when $n=H_m$. To explain this approximation, expand the exponent of $W_{m,n}$ as $\pi\sum_{j\le n}u_{m,j}\epsilon_{n-j}$. For $j>H_m$, replace $u_{m,j}$ by its integer trace $T_{m,j}$; the resulting factor is deterministic because $e^{i\pi T_{m,j}\epsilon_{n-j}}=(-1)^{T_{m,j}}$. For $j<-H_m$, drop the term. The remaining indices are exactly those in <a href="#sal:window-phase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:window-phase">[sal:window-phase]</a>.

<a id="sal:window-error"></a>
The difference of the two phase angles, after these replacements, is a sum of independent mean-zero signs. The inequality $|e^{ix}-e^{iy}|\le|x-y|$ therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \|W_{m,n}-\widetilde W_{m,n}\|_2^2
 &\le\pi^2\left(
       \sum_{j=H_m+1}^n|u_{m,j}-T_{m,j}|^2
                  +\sum_{j<-H_m}|u_{m,j}|^2\right)\notag\\
 &\le C\left(\beta^{2(b_m-H_m)}+n\delta_m^2\right)
 \le Ce^{-L_m/2}.\label{sal:window-error}
\end{aligned}$$ Here <a href="#sal:trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:trace">[sal:trace]</a> bounds the first summand by a geometric tail and a contribution $C n\delta_m^2$; the other sum is itself a geometric tail. Finally, $D\log\beta=10$ and $n\le J_m$ give the last inequality. All norms in <a href="#sal:window-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:window-error">[sal:window-error]</a> refer to the sign probability space. The windows belonging to consecutive members of $\mathcal N_m$ are disjoint, so the variables $\widetilde W_{m,n}$ are independent.

<details id="proof-stmt-sal-loss-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-sal-loss</summary>

<a id="sal:concentration"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#sal:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:main">11</a>.* For $n\in\mathcal N_m$, let $\sigma_{m,n}\in\{-1,1\}$ be the sign of the real expectation in <a href="#sal:expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:expectation">[sal:expectation]</a>. Define the continuous function $$F_m(y)=\frac1{M_m}\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_m}
                       \sigma_{m,n}e^{i\pi A_m\beta^ny}.$$ Its mean at $Y$ is real and, by <a href="#sal:mean-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:mean-lower">[sal:mean-lower]</a>, $a_m:=\mathbb EF_m(Y)\ge e^{-L_m/20}$. The corresponding average of the independent variables $\sigma_{m,n}\widetilde W_{m,n}$ has centered $L^2$ norm at most $M_m^{-1/2}$, since each variable has modulus one. By the triangle inequality and <a href="#sal:window-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:window-error">[sal:window-error]</a>, $$\label{sal:concentration}
 \|F_m(Y)-a_m\|_2
       \le M_m^{-1/2}+Ce^{-L_m/4}.$$ Subtracting the means of the approximation errors introduces at most a factor two, included in $C$.

On the other hand, the same average is small in Lebesgue $L^2(I)$. Write its frequencies in increasing order as $\xi_1<\cdots<\xi_{M_m}$. Since $A_m\ge1$ and their exponents are distinct nonnegative integers, $\xi_{r+1}-\xi_r\ge\pi(\beta-1)$. Thus $|\xi_r-\xi_s|\ge\pi(\beta-1)|r-s|$ and $$\left|\int_I e^{i(\xi_r-\xi_s)y}\,dy\right|
       \le\frac2{|\xi_r-\xi_s|}\quad(r\ne s).$$ Expanding the square and summing by the rank difference yields <a id="sal:lebesgue-average"></a>$$\label{sal:lebesgue-average}
 \int_I|F_m(y)|^2\,dy
       \le C\frac{1+\log M_m}{M_m}.$$

Consider the compact sets $$E_m=\{y\in I:\operatorname{Re}F_m(y)\ge\tfrac12e^{-L_m/20}\}.$$ Chebyshev’s inequality, <a href="#sal:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:concentration">[sal:concentration]</a>, and <a href="#sal:block-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sal:block-count">[sal:block-count]</a> imply $$1-\nu_{1/\beta}(E_m)
 \le4e^{L_m/10}\left(M_m^{-1/2}+Ce^{-L_m/4}\right)^2
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Likewise, writing $|E_m|$ for Lebesgue measure, $$|E_m|\le4e^{L_m/10}\int_I|F_m(y)|^2\,dy
       \le C e^{L_m/10}\frac{1+\log M_m}{M_m}
       \longrightarrow0,$$ because $M_m\ge e^{.7L_m}$ and $\log M_m\le1.5L_m+O(1)$. Choose a subsequence $(m_r)$ along which $\sum_r(|E_{m_r}|+1-\nu_{1/\beta}(E_{m_r}))<\infty$. Then $E=\limsup_r E_{m_r}$ is Borel and Lebesgue null, since $|\bigcup_{r\ge R}E_{m_r}|\le\sum_{r\ge R}|E_{m_r}|\to0$. Moreover, the summability of the complementary probabilities shows that $\nu_{1/\beta}$-almost every point lies in all sufficiently late $E_{m_r}$, and hence in $E$. Therefore $\nu_{1/\beta}(E)=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sal:examples-section"></a>
#### Two explicit parameters

<a id="stmt-sal-examples"></a>
<div id="sal:examples" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14**. *Let $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$ be the respective real roots greater than one of $$P_1(x)=x^4-x^3-x^2-x+1,
 \qquad P_2(x)=x^4-2x^3+x^2-2x+1.$$ Then $\beta_1,\beta_2\in(1,2)$ and both $\nu_{1/\beta_1}$ and $\nu_{1/\beta_2}$ are singular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sal-examples"><summary>Proof of stmt-sal-examples</summary>

*Proof.* For $y=x+x^{-1}$, the equations $P_i(x)/x^2=0$ become, respectively, $$y^2-y-3=0,\qquad y^2-2y-1=0.$$ Their larger roots are $y_1=(1+\sqrt{13})/2$ and $y_2=1+\sqrt2$, both in $(2,5/2)$. For each $i$, the equation $x+x^{-1}=y_i$ has one root $\beta_i\in(1,2)$ and its reciprocal. The other quadratic root $y_i'$ lies in $(-2,2)$, so the remaining two roots of $P_i$ form a nonreal conjugate pair on the unit circle.

It remains to check that these four roots are algebraic conjugates. The quadratic field $\mathbb Q(y_i)$ is real, and the discriminant $y_i^2-4$ has negative image $(y_i')^2-4$ under its nontrivial embedding. It cannot be a square in that field, since the image of a square under a real embedding is nonnegative. Hence $x^2-y_ix+1$ is irreducible over $\mathbb Q(y_i)$, and $[\mathbb Q(\beta_i):\mathbb Q]=4$. Thus $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$ are degree-four Salem numbers, and Theorem <a href="#sal:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sal:main">11</a> applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="np:section"></a>
### A polynomial with a weakly expanding conjugate pair

The final example uses a polynomial with one conjugate pair just outside the unit circle. We will prove singularity directly by counting typical prefixes. Counting all polynomial values in their conjugate coordinates therefore gives slightly too many possible prefixes. We obtain the required improvement by a product of positive trigonometric factors: its integral in the expanding coordinates stays bounded, whereas it grows exponentially on a set of words whose probability tends to one.

<a id="np:polynomial"></a>
<a id="np:relation"></a>
Throughout this section, every terminating decimal denotes the corresponding exact rational number. Put $$p(t)=t^{31}-2t^{30}+\sum_{j=0}^{9}(t^{3j+1}-t^{3j}).
 \label{np:polynomial}$$ The identity $$(t^2+t+1)p(t)=t^{33}-t^{32}-t^{31}-t^{30}-1
 \label{np:relation}$$ will control the near-cancellations in the trigonometric product.

<a id="stmt-np-main"></a>
<div id="np:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 15**. *The polynomial $p$ in <a href="#np:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:polynomial">[np:polynomial]</a> has a unique real root $\beta\in(1.8392,1.8393)$. This root is not a Pisot number, and $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.*

</div>

We first record the root bounds needed in the proof. The proposition is proved by rational root disks in Section <a href="#cert:roots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:roots">7.1</a>.

<a id="stmt-np-root-data"></a>
<div id="np:root-data" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Root bounds). *<span id="np:roots" label="np:roots"></span>All roots of $p$ are simple. Its roots outside the unit circle are $\beta,\alpha,\overline\alpha$, where $\beta$ is real, $\operatorname{Im}\alpha>0$, and <a id="np:root-bounds"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
  1.8392<\beta<1.8393,\qquad
  1.0002089<|\alpha|^2<1.0002094,\\
  |\alpha|^{-1}<0.9999.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{np:root-bounds}$$ The remaining roots are fourteen nonreal conjugate pairs, each of modulus less than $0.994$.*

</div>

<a id="np:mahler"></a>
Write $D=\{\beta,\alpha,\overline\alpha\}$ and let $I$ be the set of remaining roots. The expanding volume factor is $$\mathcal M=\prod_{r\in D}|r|=\beta|\alpha|^2,
 \qquad \log\mathcal M<\log\beta+0.0002094.
 \label{np:mahler}$$ The last inequality uses $\log(1+u)<u$ for $u>0$.

The root bounds already show that $\beta$ is not Pisot. Indeed, suppose $\beta$ were Pisot, and divide $p$ by its monic minimal polynomial. The quotient is monic and integral, has constant term of absolute value one, and contains $\alpha,\overline\alpha$ among its roots but not $\beta$. The product of the moduli of its roots is greater than one if it has no roots in $I$, and otherwise is at most $1.0002094\cdot0.994<1$. Both conclusions contradict the constant term. It remains to prove singularity.

<a id="np:characters-section"></a>
#### Word vectors and characters

<a id="np:word-coordinates"></a>
For a word $a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{N-1})\in\{0,1\}^N$, define $$y_r(a)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_jr^j\quad(r\in D\cup I),
 \qquad x_r(a)=r^{-N}y_r(a)\quad(r\in D).
 \label{np:word-coordinates}$$ Vectors indexed by roots are always constrained by $y_{\overline r}=\overline{y_r}$, and a conjugate pair counts as one complex coordinate, or two real coordinates. In particular, the space of all $y$-vectors has real dimension $31$.

<a id="stmt-np-lattice"></a>
<div id="np:lattice" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *All vectors $y(a)$, for all $N$ and all words $a$, belong to a fixed full lattice in this real vector space. Their contracting coordinates $(y_r)_{r\in I}$ and normalized expanding coordinates $(x_r)_{r\in D}$ lie in fixed bounded boxes. The change from $(x_D,y_I)$ to $(y_D,y_I)$ has real Jacobian determinant of absolute value $\mathcal M^N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-lattice"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-lattice</summary>

*Proof.* Reduction of $\sum a_jt^j$ modulo the monic polynomial $p$ gives an integer coefficient vector of length $31$. Evaluation at the roots is an invertible real-linear map: a polynomial of degree at most $30$ that vanishes at all $31$ distinct roots is zero. The image of $\mathbb Z^{31}$ under this map is the required lattice. No irreducibility assumption is needed. The bounds follow from $$|y_r(a)|\le\frac1{1-|r|}\quad(r\in I),\qquad
 |x_r(a)|\le\frac1{|r|-1}\quad(r\in D).$$ Multiplication of the real coordinate by $\beta^N$ and of the complex coordinate by $\alpha^N$ has determinant $\beta^N|\alpha|^{2N}=\mathcal M^N$. ◻

</details>

<a id="np:coefficients"></a>
To distinguish the word vectors from typical points of these boxes, define the real sequences $$C_0(m)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\sum_{r\in I}\frac{r^{-1-m}}{p'(r)},&m<0,\\[4pt]
  \displaystyle-\sum_{r\in D}\frac{r^{-1-m}}{p'(r)},&m\ge0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad C_1(m)=C_0(m+3)-C_0(m)+C_0(m-1).
 \label{np:coefficients}$$ Partial fractions give $$\frac1{p(t)}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}C_0(m)t^m,
 \qquad \max_{r\in I}|r|<|t|<\min_{r\in D}|r|.$$ Thus $C_0$ is the bilateral Laurent coefficient sequence of $1/p$, and both $C_0,C_1$ decay exponentially at both ends. The same inversion by an exponentially decaying two-sided sequence is used by Lind and Schmidt (Lind and Schmidt 1999, Lemma 4.5 and Example 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[LindSchmidt1999](#ref-LindSchmidt1999)] to construct homoclinic points for algebraic actions. Here the explicit root formula will give characters adapted to the word vectors.

<a id="np:characters"></a>
For arbitrary expanding coordinates $x_D$, with $x_\beta\in\mathbb R$ and $x_{\overline\alpha}=\overline{x_\alpha}$, put $$\begin{split}
 Z_i^{(0,N)}(x)
 &=\exp\!\left(-2\pi\mathrm i\sum_{r\in D}
                   \frac{x_rr^i}{p'(r)}\right)
   \exp\!\left(-\pi\mathrm i\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}C_0(k-i)\right),\\
 Z_i^{(1,N)}(x)
 &=Z_{i-3}^{(0,N)}(x)\overline{Z_i^{(0,N)}(x)}Z_{i+1}^{(0,N)}(x),
 \qquad i\in\mathbb Z.
 \end{split}
 \label{np:characters}$$ These functions have modulus one.

<a id="stmt-np-word-character"></a>
<div id="np:word-character" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Characters at word vectors). *If $b_k=a_{N-1-k}$, then for $A\in\{0,1\}$ and $i\in\mathbb Z$, <a id="np:word-character-formula"></a>$$Z_i^{(A,N)}(x(a))
 =\exp\!\left(\pi\mathrm i\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}
                     C_A(k-i)(2b_k-1)\right).
 \label{np:word-character-formula}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-word-character"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-word-character</summary>

*Proof.* The reversed word gives $x_r(a)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}b_kr^{-1-k}$. For each integer $l\ge0$, Lagrange interpolation shows that $$\sum_{r\in D\cup I}\frac{r^l}{p'(r)}$$ is the coefficient of $t^{30}$ in the remainder of $t^l$ modulo $p$, and hence is an integer. Consequently, when $m<0$, the coefficient $-\sum_{r\in D}r^{-1-m}/p'(r)$ differs from $C_0(m)$ by an integer. Its contribution to the exponent with coefficient $2\pi\mathrm i b_k$ is unchanged by this replacement. When $m\ge0$, the coefficients already agree. The second factor in <a href="#np:characters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:characters">[np:characters]</a> supplies the centering by $-1$, proving the formula for $A=0$. Multiplication of the three characters proves it for $A=1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="np:stationary"></a>
The corresponding stationary model uses independent fair bits $(b_k)_{k\in\mathbb Z}$ and the unit random variables $$\mathcal Z_i^{(A)}=
 \exp\!\left(\pi\mathrm i\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}C_A(k-i)(2b_k-1)\right).
 \label{np:stationary}$$ The series converges absolutely. Independence gives the real moments <a id="np:scalar-products"></a>$$\begin{split}
 P_A&=\mathbb E\mathcal Z_i^{(A)}
       =\prod_{m\in\mathbb Z}\cos(\pi C_A(m)),\\
 P_{AB}^{\pm}(k)
   &=\mathbb E\bigl[\mathcal Z_i^{(A)}
                    (\mathcal Z_{i-k}^{(B)})^{\pm1}\bigr]\\
   &=\prod_{m\in\mathbb Z}\cos\bigl(\pi(C_A(m)\pm C_B(m+k))\bigr),
 \qquad k\ge0.
 \end{split}
 \label{np:scalar-products}$$

<a id="np:predictor-section"></a>
#### A predictor with a positive mean gain

<a id="np:predictor"></a>
The weakly expanding pair produces long decaying tails in $C_0$. Some shifted tails nearly cancel in the signed products $P_{00}^{\pm}(k)$. A linear combination of the corresponding phases will use these correlations. For rational weights $w_{ks}$, define $$\begin{split}
 G_i^{(N)}(x)
  &=-0.001+0.0055 Z_{i-33}^{(1,N)}(x)
    +\sum_{k=30}^{2000}\sum_{s=0}^1
           w_{ks}\bigl(Z_{i-k}^{(0,N)}(x)\bigr)^{1-2s},\\
 \mathcal G_i
  &=-0.001+0.0055\mathcal Z_{i-33}^{(1)}
    +\sum_{k=30}^{2000}\sum_{s=0}^1
           w_{ks}(\mathcal Z_{i-k}^{(0)})^{1-2s},\\
 \mathcal L_i&=\operatorname{Re}(\mathcal Z_i^{(0)}\mathcal G_i).
 \end{split}
 \label{np:predictor}$$

<a id="stmt-np-moment-bounds"></a>
<div id="np:moment-bounds" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (Predictor estimates). *<span id="np:moments" label="np:moments"></span>There exist weights $w_{ks}\in10^{-9}\mathbb Z$, indexed by $30\le k\le2000$ and $s\in\{0,1\}$, such that <a id="np:weight-sum"></a>$$0.001+0.0055+\sum_{k,s}|w_{ks}|
   =\frac{198442946}{10^9}<x_*:=0.199
 \label{np:weight-sum}$$ and, in the stationary model, <a id="np:moment-estimates"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \mathbb E\mathcal L_i>0.000210,\qquad
 \mathbb E|\mathcal G_i|^2<0.000145,\\
 \left|\mathbb E\bigl[(\mathcal Z_i^{(0)})^2\mathcal G_i^2\bigr]\right|
     <0.000007.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{np:moment-estimates}$$ In particular, $|G_i^{(N)}(x)|\le x_*$ for every $N,i,x$, and $|\mathcal G_i|\le x_*$ for every bit sequence.*

</div>

We fix weights supplied by Proposition <a href="#np:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:moments">[np:moments]</a>. Their exact rational construction is given in Section <a href="#cert:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:algorithm">7.2</a>, and the moment bounds are proved in Section <a href="#cert:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moments">7.4</a>.

The next two subsections construct a positive product whose logarithm has a positive mean under this stationary model and whose integral is bounded in the expanding coordinates. These two estimates will remove an exponential proportion of the lattice points allowed by Lemma <a href="#np:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:lattice">17</a>.

<a id="np:damping-section"></a>
#### Damping the possible frequency cancellations

Choose a fixed integer $i_0\ge1$ so that <a id="np:initial-index"></a>$$\frac{0.1\beta^{i_0}}{|p'(\beta)|}>1.
 \label{np:initial-index}$$ For a word length $N$, use every complete block $$B_q=\{i_0+22q,\ldots,i_0+22q+20\}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,N-1\},
 \qquad q\ge0,$$ and write $J_N$ for their union. Thus each block has $21$ indices and consecutive blocks are separated by one unused index.

<a id="np:near-cubic"></a>
<a id="np:state-values"></a>
The near-relation <a href="#np:relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:relation">[np:relation]</a> dictates which signed digit patterns need to be retained when integrating a block. For a pattern $(d_i)_{i\in B_q}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{B_q}$, let $b=\max B_q$ and set $$v_i=\beta^{-i}\sum_{j=i}^{b}d_j\beta^j,\qquad
 v_{b+1}=0.$$ Reading from the largest index downwards gives $v_i=\beta v_{i+1}+d_i$. At the root $\beta$, <a href="#np:relation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:relation">[np:relation]</a> becomes $$\beta^3-\beta^2-\beta-1=\beta^{-30}.
 \label{np:near-cubic}$$ In particular, the four digits $[\sigma,-\sigma,-\sigma,-\sigma]$ leave the small normalized remainder $\sigma\beta^{-30}$. We encode these approximate returns by the transitions in Figure <a href="#np:automaton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:automaton">1</a>. The initial state is $0$, and the permitted terminal states are $0,C_+,C_-$. For each sign $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$, the numerical values assigned to the states are $$0,\qquad A_\sigma:\ \sigma,\qquad
 B_\sigma:\ \sigma(\beta-1),\qquad
 C_\sigma:\ \sigma(\beta^2-\beta-1).
 \label{np:state-values}$$ A pattern is called *admissible* if every transition is allowed and its terminal state is permitted.

<figure id="np:automaton">

<figcaption>Admissible digits, read from high exponents to low exponents. There are two copies of the three nonzero states, one for each sign $σ$, sharing state $0$. Edge labels are digits; double circles mark permitted terminal states. A return to $0$ may be followed by either sign.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-np-automaton"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`np:automaton`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, every node/.style={font=\small}]
  \node[draw,circle,double,minimum size=9mm] (z) at (0,0) {$0$};
  \node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (a) at (2.4,0) {$A_\sigma$};
  \node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (b) at (4.8,0) {$B_\sigma$};
  \node[draw,circle,double,minimum size=9mm] (c) at (7.2,0) {$C_\sigma$};
  \draw[->] (-1,0) -- (z);
  \draw[->] (z) edge[loop above] node {$0$} (z);
  \draw[->] (z) -- node[above] {$\sigma$} (a);
  \draw[->] (a) -- node[above] {$-\sigma$} (b);
  \draw[->] (b) -- node[above] {$-\sigma$} (c);
  \draw[->] (c.north) .. controls +(0,1.15) and +(0,1.15) ..
             node[above] {$0$} (a.north);
  \draw[->] (c.south) .. controls +(0,-1.35) and +(0,-1.35) ..
             node[below] {$-\sigma$} (z.south);
 \end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 21 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
State-transition diagram with four states horizontally aligned from left to right: 0, A_\sigma, B_\sigma, and C_\sigma. Nodes 0 and C_\sigma are drawn with double circles marking permitted terminal states, while A_\sigma and B_\sigma are drawn with single circles. Directed edges are: a self-loop on 0 labeled 0 on top; a directed horizontal arrow from 0 to A_\sigma labeled \sigma; a directed horizontal arrow from A_\sigma to B_\sigma labeled -\sigma; a directed horizontal arrow from B_\sigma to C_\sigma labeled -\sigma; an upper curved arrow looping from B_\sigma back to 0 labeled 0; and a lower curved arrow looping from C_\sigma back to 0 labeled -\sigma.
```


<a id="np:damping"></a>
Let $o(d)=\#\{i:d_i\ne0\}$. At a position $i$ of a block, define $$\eta_i=\sum_{\substack{d\text{ admissible}\\d_i\ne0}}
                  \frac{x_*^{o(d)-2}}{o(d)}.
 \label{np:damping}$$ The same coefficients are repeated in every block. An admissible nonzero pattern has at least three nonzero digits, so all summands are well defined.

<a id="stmt-np-damping-bounds"></a>
<div id="np:damping-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="np:pattern-cost"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Cost of admissible patterns). *Set $$S_*=\left(1+\frac{2x_*^4}{1-x_*^2}\right)^{18}
       \left(1+\frac{2x_*^3}{1-x_*^2}\right)-1.
 \label{np:pattern-cost}$$ Then $S_*<0.081$, and <a id="np:damping-estimates"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \sum_{\substack{d\text{ admissible}\\o(d)>0}}x_*^{o(d)}\le S_*,
 \qquad \eta_i x_*^2\le S_*/3,\\
 \frac{\overline\eta}{1-S_*/3}<0.101,
 \qquad \overline\eta=\frac1{21}\sum_{i\in B_q}\eta_i.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{np:damping-estimates}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-damping-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-damping-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* A completed excursion from $0$ to $0$ has the digit string $$[\sigma,-\sigma,-\sigma,(0,-\sigma,-\sigma)^l,-\sigma],
 \qquad l\ge0.$$ It has $4+2l$ nonzero digits and can begin in at most $18$ positions of a block. An optional final excursion ending at $C_\sigma$ has the same form without the last digit; its sign and length determine its starting position. Ignoring incompatibilities between these choices overcounts the admissible patterns and gives <a href="#np:pattern-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:pattern-cost">[np:pattern-cost]</a>. At $x_*=0.199$, the two fractions in that expression are less than $0.00327$ and $0.01642$. The inequality $(1+a)^{18}\le(1-18a)^{-1}$, valid for $0\le18a<1$, proves $S_*<0.081$ by rational arithmetic.

Since $o(d)\ge3$, each $\eta_i x_*^2\le S_*/3$. Summing <a href="#np:damping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:damping">[np:damping]</a> over positions gives $\sum_i\eta_i\le S_*/x_*^2$. Thus $$\frac{\overline\eta}{1-S_*/3}
 \le\frac{0.081}{21(0.199)^2(1-0.081/3)}<0.101.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="np:factors"></a>
For the moment fix $N$, and abbreviate $Z_i=Z_i^{(0,N)}$, $G_i=G_i^{(N)}$, and $x_i=|G_i|$. Define $$h_i=1-\eta_i x_i^2,\qquad
 F_i=h_i\bigl(1+(1-x_i^2)(Z_iG_i+\overline{Z_iG_i})\bigr),
 \qquad i\in J_N.
 \label{np:factors}$$ These factors are bounded above and bounded away from zero uniformly in $N,i,x_D$. Indeed, $h_i\ge1-S_*/3$, and the other factor is at least $1-2x_*>0$.

<a id="stmt-np-integral"></a>
<div id="np:integral" class="proposition">

<a id="np:integral-bound"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Bounded integral). *For every $N$ and every fixed complex coordinate $x_\alpha$, $$\int_\mathbb R\psi(x_\beta)\prod_{i\in J_N}F_i(x_D)\,dx_\beta
 \le\int_\mathbb R\psi(x_\beta)\,dx_\beta,
 \qquad
 \psi(v)=\left(\frac{\sin(\pi v/10)}{\pi v/10}\right)^2,
 \label{np:integral-bound}$$ where $\psi(0)=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-integral"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-integral</summary>

*Proof.* We use the Fourier convention $e^{2\pi\mathrm i\xi x_\beta}$. The nonnegative integrable function $\psi$ has Fourier transform supported in $[-0.1,0.1]$, hence in $[-1,1]$. On suppressing the common frequency factor $-1/p'(\beta)$, the frequency of $Z_i$ is $\beta^i$. Every phase appearing in $G_i$ has frequency of absolute value at most $\beta^{i-30}$. For the type-1 term this follows from $$\bigl|\beta^{i-33}(\beta^{-3}-1+\beta)\bigr|\le\beta^{i-30}.$$ We call these predictor frequencies slow. Expanding a factor $F_i$ gives monomials with one fast digit $d_i\in\{-1,0,1\}$, corresponding to $Z_i^{d_i}$, and at most five slow frequencies: two from $h_i$, two from $1-|G_i|^2$, and one from $G_i$ or its conjugate.

<a id="np:recurrence-error"></a>
Expand the last block according to its fast digits, keeping all earlier factors for the moment. We first show that every inadmissible pattern has zero integral, even after multiplication by those earlier factors. Expand the latter and all slow pieces into monomials. If the current index is $i$, the fast sum accumulated from the top of the last block, divided by $\beta^i$, follows the recurrence $v\mapsto\beta v+d_i$. By <a href="#np:near-cubic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:near-cubic">[np:near-cubic]</a>, each allowed transition agrees with the state values <a href="#np:state-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:state-values">[np:state-values]</a> up to an injected error of absolute value at most $\beta^{-30}$. The propagated error, including at a first forbidden step, is bounded by $$\frac{\beta^{-30}\beta^{21}}{\beta-1}<0.005.
 \label{np:recurrence-error}$$

At a first forbidden step, the ideal absolute value is at least $\beta(\beta-1)$ for a departure from $A_\sigma$ or $B_\sigma$, and at least $2$ for a departure from $C_\sigma$. After <a href="#np:recurrence-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:recurrence-error">[np:recurrence-error]</a>, it is greater than $1.5$. All lower fast digits together contribute at most $(\beta-1)^{-1}<1.192$ in the same units. If instead all transitions are allowed but the terminal state is $A_\sigma$ or $B_\sigma$, the absolute value at the bottom index $i$ is at least $\beta-1-0.005$. The one-index gap then bounds the earlier fast terms by $1/(\beta(\beta-1))<0.649$.

In either case, all slow terms, including those from earlier blocks, contribute at most <a id="np:slow-error"></a>$$\frac{5\beta^{-30}\beta^{21}}{\beta-1}<0.025
 \label{np:slow-error}$$ relative to $\beta^i$. To see this, sum at most five frequencies of size $\beta^{j-30}$ over indices $j$ no larger than the top of the last block; that top is at most $i+20$. Thus the full frequency has absolute value greater than $0.1\beta^i/|p'(\beta)|>1$. Its integral against $\psi$ is zero.

After removing these zero integrals, the last block contributes $\prod_{i\in B_q}h_i$ times the sum over admissible fast patterns. The zero pattern contributes one. A nonzero pattern has absolute value at most $\prod_{d_i\ne0}x_i$, since $0\le1-x_i^2\le1$. The arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives $$\prod_{d_i\ne0}x_i
 \le x_*^{o(d)-2}\frac1{o(d)}\sum_{d_i\ne0}x_i^2.$$ The absolute value of the retained pattern sum is therefore at most $1+\sum_{i\in B_q}\eta_i x_i^2$. Writing $t_i=\eta_i x_i^2$, we have $0\le t_i<1$ and $$\prod_{i\in B_q}(1-t_i)\left(1+\sum_{i\in B_q}t_i\right)\le1;$$ for example, use $\prod(1-t_i)\le\exp(-\sum t_i)$. Since $\psi$ and all earlier factors are nonnegative, removing the last block can only increase the upper bound for the integral. Repeat with the preceding blocks to obtain <a href="#np:integral-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:integral-bound">[np:integral-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="np:gain-section"></a>
#### A typical exponential gain

We now evaluate the same factors in the stationary model, replacing $Z_i,G_i$ in <a href="#np:factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:factors">[np:factors]</a> by $\mathcal Z_i^{(0)},\mathcal G_i$; denote the resulting factors by $\mathcal F_i$. The damping coefficients remain periodic with period $22$, and are used at the same $21$ positions per period.

<a id="stmt-np-log-inequality"></a>
<div id="np:log-inequality" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (A logarithmic estimate). *For $0\le x\le x_*$ and $|u|\le2x$, <a id="np:log-bound"></a>$$\log(1+(1-x^2)u)\ge u-\frac{u^2}{2}-C_*x^3,
 \qquad C_*:=\frac2{3\sqrt{1-x_*^2}}.
 \label{np:log-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-log-inequality"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-log-inequality</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate for $x=0$. Otherwise let $D_x(u)=u-u^2/2-\log(1+(1-x^2)u)$. Its derivative is $$D_x'(u)=\frac{x^2-x^2u-(1-x^2)u^2}{1+(1-x^2)u}.$$ For $u\ge0$, this is at most the positive part of $x^2-(1-x^2)u^2$. Integrating that positive part on $[0,\infty)$ gives $2x^3/(3\sqrt{1-x^2})\le C_*x^3$. On $[-2x,0]$, the only possible interior critical point is a minimum, so the maximum is at an endpoint. We have $D_x(0)=0$, whereas $$\frac{d}{dx}D_x(-2x)
 =\frac{2x^2(1-2x-4x^2)}{1-2x+2x^3}\le2x^2.$$ As $D_0(0)=0$, integration gives $D_x(-2x)\le2x^3/3\le C_*x^3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-np-typical-gain"></a>
<div id="np:typical-gain" class="proposition">

<a id="np:typical-gain-bound"></a>
**Proposition 23** (Growth on typical words). *For uniformly distributed words $a\in\{0,1\}^N$, $$\Pr\left\{\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x(a))>0.000220N\right\}
 \longrightarrow1.
 \label{np:typical-gain-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-typical-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-typical-gain</summary>

<a id="np:mean-gain"></a>
*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#np:log-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:log-inequality">22</a> with $x=|\mathcal G_i|$ and $u=2\mathcal L_i$. The identity $$2\mathbb E\mathcal L_i^2
 =\mathbb E|\mathcal G_i|^2+
   \operatorname{Re}\mathbb E\bigl[(\mathcal Z_i^{(0)})^2\mathcal G_i^2\bigr]$$ and the bound $\mathbb E|\mathcal G_i|^3\le x_*\mathbb E|\mathcal G_i|^2$ control the second and third order terms. Also $$\log(1-\eta_i|\mathcal G_i|^2)
 \ge-\frac{\eta_i|\mathcal G_i|^2}{1-S_*/3}.$$ Set $\gamma=22^{-1}\sum_{i\in B_0}\mathbb E\log\mathcal F_i$, where the division by $22$ includes the unused index. Since $C_*x_*<0.136$, Proposition <a href="#np:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:moments">[np:moments]</a> and Lemma <a href="#np:damping-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:damping-bounds">20</a> give the stationary mean rate $\gamma$ with $$\begin{split}
 \gamma
 &\ge\frac{21}{22}\left(
     2\mathbb E\mathcal L_0
     -\left(1+C_*x_*+\frac{\overline\eta}{1-S_*/3}\right)
            \mathbb E|\mathcal G_0|^2
     -\left|\mathbb E[(\mathcal Z_0^{(0)})^2\mathcal G_0^2]\right|
                       \right)\\
 &>\frac{21}{22}\bigl(2(0.000210)-1.237(0.000145)-0.000007\bigr)
   >0.000222.
 \end{split}
 \label{np:mean-gain}$$

For completeness, we justify passage from this mean to finite words. Truncate each sequence $C_A$ to $[-H,H]$, for a fixed integer $H$. The stationary logarithmic block sums formed from these truncated sequences depend on finitely many bits. They are uniformly bounded, have the same mean from block to block, and are independent when their blocks are sufficiently far apart. The variance of an average of $Q$ such block sums is therefore $O_H(Q^{-1})$. Their averages converge in probability to their means.

Absolute summability of $C_0,C_1$ makes the truncated characters uniformly close to the original characters as $H\to\infty$. The predictors are finite sums with bounded total absolute weight, and the factors are uniformly bounded away from zero. Consequently the logarithms also converge uniformly under this truncation. Letting $H\to\infty$ after $N\to\infty$ proves $$\frac1N\sum_{i\in J_N}\log\mathcal F_i
   \longrightarrow\gamma\qquad\text{in probability}.$$

Couple the reversed word bits to $(b_0,\ldots,b_{N-1})$ in this stationary sequence. For fixed $H$, the truncated finite and stationary characters used in a factor agree whenever $H+2000\le i\le N-1-H$. Only a number of boundary indices depending on $H$, not on $N$, remain. The same uniform approximation thus shows $$\frac1N\left(
   \sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x(a))
       -\sum_{i\in J_N}\log\mathcal F_i\right)\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly over the coupled bit sequences. Together with <a href="#np:mean-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:mean-gain">[np:mean-gain]</a>, this proves <a href="#np:typical-gain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:typical-gain-bound">[np:typical-gain-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="np:cover-section"></a>
#### Counting typical vectors and proving singularity

The integral bound applies to continuous coordinates, whereas Proposition <a href="#np:typical-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:typical-gain">23</a> concerns lattice points. The remaining step is to thicken each such point by a fixed radius. Normalization in the expanding directions makes the resulting change in the whole logarithmic product bounded independently of the word length.

<a id="stmt-np-thickening"></a>
<div id="np:thickening" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Thickening word vectors). *There are constants $\rho>0$ and $C_{\mathrm{thick}}<\infty$, independent of $N$, such that the radius-$\rho$ balls about distinct lattice points in Lemma <a href="#np:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:lattice">17</a> are disjoint, and, whenever $y'$ lies in the ball about $y(a)$, <a id="np:thickening-bound"></a>$$\left|\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x'_D)
       -\sum_{i\in J_N}\log F_i(x_D(a))\right|\le C_{\mathrm{thick}},
 \qquad x'_r=r^{-N}y'_r\quad(r\in D).
 \label{np:thickening-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-thickening"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-thickening</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\rho\le1$ smaller than half the minimum distance between distinct points of the lattice. A coordinate change of size at most one in $y$ changes the exponent of $Z_j^{(0,N)}$ by at most $$2\pi\sum_{r\in D}\frac{|r|^{j-N}}{|p'(r)|}.$$ Every character occurring in $F_i$, including those in the type-1 term of the predictor, has index at most $i$. The logarithm of $F_i$ is a uniformly Lipschitz function of these unit phases, since the coefficient sums and damping coefficients are bounded and $F_i$ is uniformly positive. Its variation is therefore at most a fixed constant times $\sum_{r\in D}|r|^{i-N}$. Finally, $$\sum_{i\in J_N}|r|^{i-N}
 \le\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}|r|^{i-N}
 <\frac1{|r|-1}\qquad(r\in D).$$ This proves a bound independent of $N$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-np-thickening-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-np-thickening</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#np:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:main">15</a>.* The root assertion and the fact that $\beta$ is not Pisot were proved above. Call a word typical if it satisfies the inequality in <a href="#np:typical-gain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:typical-gain-bound">[np:typical-gain-bound]</a>, and let $\mathcal Y_N$ be the set of distinct vectors $y(a)$ obtained from typical words. Typical words have probability tending to one. Lemma <a href="#np:thickening" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:thickening">24</a> implies that, for all sufficiently large $N$, the product $\prod_{i\in J_N}F_i$ is at least $\exp(0.000216N)$ throughout each of the disjoint balls about $\mathcal Y_N$.

<a id="np:count"></a>
All these balls lie in fixed bounded boxes in $(x_D,y_I)$ coordinates, enlarged from those of Lemma <a href="#np:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:lattice">17</a>. In the real expanding coordinate one may take $|x_\beta|\le3$. The function $\psi$ has a positive minimum on $[-3,3]$. Integrating first in $x_\beta$, Proposition <a href="#np:integral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:integral">21</a> bounds the integral of the product over this fixed box by a constant independent of $N$. The product does not depend on $y_I$. Changing back to $y$ coordinates contributes the Jacobian $\mathcal M^N$. Comparing with the disjoint balls, each of the same positive volume, gives $$\#\mathcal Y_N\le C_{\mathrm{count}}
       \exp\bigl((\log\mathcal M-0.000216)N\bigr)
 \label{np:count}$$ for a constant $C_{\mathrm{count}}$.

Let $\mu$ be the law of $\sum_{k\ge0}b_k\beta^{-k}$ for independent fair bits. By reversal in <a href="#np:word-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:word-coordinates">[np:word-coordinates]</a>, its length-$N$ prefix has the distribution of $\beta x_\beta(a)$. Projecting $\mathcal Y_N$ to this coordinate cannot increase its cardinality. For each resulting prefix value, attach an interval of length $\beta^{-N}/(1-\beta^{-1})$, which contains all possible remaining tails. Their union $K_N$ is compact, $\mu(K_N)\to1$, and <a href="#np:mahler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:mahler">[np:mahler]</a> and <a href="#np:count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:count">[np:count]</a> give <a id="np:cover-bound"></a>$$|K_N|\le C_{\mathrm{cover}}\exp\bigl((0.0002094-0.000216)N\bigr)
       =C_{\mathrm{cover}}e^{-0.0000066N}.
 \label{np:cover-bound}$$ Choose a subsequence $N_j$ with $\mu(K_{N_j})>1-2^{-j}$. Then $\liminf_jK_{N_j}$ has full $\mu$-measure and zero Lebesgue measure. Thus $\mu$ is singular. Finally, $$\sum_{k\ge0}(2b_k-1)\beta^{-k}
 =2\sum_{k\ge0}b_k\beta^{-k}-\frac1{1-\beta^{-1}},$$ so its law $\nu_{1/\beta}$ is an affine image of $\mu$ and is singular as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:section"></a>
### A rational certificate for the degree-31 example

This appendix proves the root bounds in Proposition <a href="#np:roots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:roots">[np:roots]</a> and the predictor estimates in Proposition <a href="#np:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:moments">[np:moments]</a>. The root calculation uses exact rational arithmetic. The moment calculation combines finite rational products with an analytic estimate for their infinite tails. We specify the rounding and the order of operations so that the rational weights in <a href="#cert:weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:weights">[cert:weights]</a> are completely determined. The accompanying script `verification/verify_degree31.py` implements these instructions using only Python integers and rational fractions; the recurrences below give the complete certificate independently of that implementation. Every terminating decimal in this appendix denotes its exact rational value.

<a id="cert:roots"></a>
#### Locating all the roots

<span id="cert:root-proof" label="cert:root-proof"></span>

Recall the polynomial $$p(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{9}(-t^{3j}+t^{3j+1})-2t^{30}+t^{31}.$$ Put $E=10^{40}$. The entries in Table <a href="#cert:root-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:root-table">1</a> are the real and imaginary numerators of centers $z=(a+\mathrm i b)/E$. Include the conjugate of each nonreal center. Thus the table specifies $31$ disks, all of radius $\delta=10^{-35}$. Denote its first and sixth centers by $z_\beta$ and $z_\alpha$, respectively.

<div id="cert:root-table">

|                            Real numerator |                      Imaginary numerator |
|------------------------------------------:|-----------------------------------------:|
| 18392867573155250211326543340571993487329 |                                        0 |
| -9741384652837567528189111907762645564212 |  960611633124778624015947511546714789832 |
| -9390979934023233037686839914141181659013 | 2848063934279278841200845525848576791934 |
| -8707010393241200595697704637503228167256 | 4634358961359777463662031763152574795910 |
| -7723118558199735333585947045021501410451 | 6250214494152701297733561366714273855663 |
| -6480873022754265587670654805348739312397 | 7617033927823246411701558316834470378693 |
| -3296776282962928450195355562079974291919 | 9362810613239308448752821856735617305389 |
| -1442513481645415489053007250852371013818 | 9733039088635544125591234293715446465839 |
|   473579005983151143865690217073943282984 | 9759329295153416086962240697595385836176 |
|  2372858204750359429049351486827380431084 | 9430908396110323024329804770889268443510 |
|  4184061662454447896993374181551299054853 | 8750376307352230997951514116951420713869 |
|  5840543823426662247726359012202878127980 | 7733999914049269380846159864123679516166 |
|  7280042164772721585016920411987150804420 | 6409668313572020921539025901166241650504 |
|  8444162922855313595050499086097221186662 | 4814843758334683329011653039104490553475 |
|  9275649862302447872891854755866108397375 | 2996533501299661135726051235425500195711 |
|  9715324892541617145821300300817663390044 | 1019107767839771580294306582422817927060 |

Rational centers for the roots; both columns have denominator $10^{40}$.

</div>

<a id="cert:residual"></a>
<a id="cert:center-bounds"></a>
For a complex number write $\|z\|_1=|\Re z|+|\Im z|$ and $\|z\|_\infty=\max(|\Re z|,|\Im z|)$. Exact Horner evaluation gives, at every displayed center, $$\label{cert:residual}
 |z|<2,\qquad \|p'(z)\|_\infty>1,\qquad
 2\cdot10^{35}\|p(z)\|_1<\|p'(z)\|_\infty.$$ Here is an integer prescription for these comparisons. For $q(t)=\sum_{j=0}^d q_jt^j$ and the Gaussian integer $Z=Ez$, initialize $(V,h)=(q_d,1)$ and, for $j=d-1,d-2,\ldots,0$, replace $$h\leftarrow Eh,\qquad V\leftarrow ZV+hq_j.$$ The final value is $q(z)=V/h$. Apply this to $p$ and $p'$, and clear the positive denominators in <a href="#cert:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual">[cert:residual]</a>. Comparisons of squared distances give $|z-z'|>.01$ for distinct centers, including conjugates. Comparisons of coordinates and squared moduli also give $$\label{cert:center-bounds}
 \begin{gathered}
 1.83928<z_\beta<1.83929,\qquad
 1.0002090<|z_\alpha|^2<1.0002093,\\
 |z|<.9939\quad\hbox{at all other displayed centers}.
 \end{gathered}$$ For $|h|=\delta$, the terms of degree at least two in $p(z+h)-p(z)-p'(z)h$ have total modulus at most $$\delta^2\sum_{j=0}^{31}|p_j|3^j<10^{17}\delta^2.$$ Indeed, $|z|<2$ and $\delta<1$, so the binomial expansion gives this bound term by term. By <a href="#cert:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual">[cert:residual]</a>, the constant term is less than $|p'(z)|\delta/2$, whereas the displayed remainder is less than $|p'(z)|\delta/2$. Rouché’s theorem therefore puts exactly one root, counted with multiplicity, in each disk. The disks are disjoint and their number is the degree, so these are all the roots and all are simple. The root in the real-centered disk is real by conjugation. Every other disk misses the real axis, so the remaining roots form fifteen nonreal conjugate pairs. The radius $10^{-35}$ and <a href="#cert:center-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:center-bounds">[cert:center-bounds]</a> imply every bound in Proposition <a href="#np:roots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:roots">[np:roots]</a>, including $|1/\alpha|<.9999$.

<a id="cert:algorithm"></a>
#### Rounded coefficient arrays and finite products

The numerical inputs are the sequences $C_0,C_1$ defined in the main text in <a href="#np:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:coefficients">[np:coefficients]</a>. We approximate their single and paired moments <a id="cert:exact-moments"></a>$$\label{cert:exact-moments}
 P_A=\prod_{m\in\mathbb Z}\cos(\pi C_A(m)),\qquad
 P_{AB}^{\sigma}(k)=
 \prod_{m\in\mathbb Z}\cos\bigl(\pi(C_A(m)+\sigma C_B(m+k))\bigr),$$ where $A,B\in\{0,1\}$, $\sigma\in\{1,-1\}$, and $k\ge0$. Both products converge by exponential decay of the coefficients. The sign notation $+$ and $-$ means $\sigma=1$ and $\sigma=-1$.

We now specify the approximations used to define the weights. Use $$S=10^{30},\qquad R=3000,\qquad H=100,\qquad K=2000,
 \qquad W=10^9.$$ For a real rational $x$ set $$[x]=\frac{\lfloor Sx+1/2\rfloor}{S},$$ and round complex numbers componentwise. Thus, if $x=u/v$ with integers $u,v$ and $v>0$, its rounded numerator is $\lfloor(2Su+v)/(2v)\rfloor$. Additions, subtractions, conjugation, and multiplication by an integer are exact unless brackets are explicitly displayed. Set $$\pi'=\frac{3141592653589793238462643383279}{S}.$$ The inequality $|\pi'-\pi|<1/S$ follows by using $30$ terms of the alternating series for $\arctan(1/5)$ and $10$ for $\arctan(1/239)$ in Machin’s identity $\pi=16\arctan(1/5)-4\arctan(1/239)$. The total remainder bound is $$\frac{16}{61\cdot5^{61}}+\frac4{21\cdot239^{21}}.$$ In particular this verification, too, uses rational arithmetic only.

<a id="cert:base-pairs"></a>
<a id="cert:shift-array"></a>
The contracting and expanding roots are denoted by $I$ and $D$, as in the main text. For each representative center $z$ first form $r_z=[1/p'(z)]$, with $p'(z)$ evaluated exactly. Store a base and a coefficient as follows: $$\label{cert:base-pairs}
 (a_z,v_z)=
 \begin{cases}
 ([z],r_z),&|z|<1,\\
 ([1/z],-[[1/z]r_z]),&|z|>1.
 \end{cases}$$ For a contracting representative, start $v=v_z$ and add $2\Re v$ to $c_0(-1)$, then to $c_0(-2),c_0(-3),\ldots$, replacing $v\leftarrow[va_z]$ after each addition. For an expanding representative do the same on $c_0(0),c_0(1),\ldots$; the multiplier is $1$ for the real root and $2$ for the nonreal representative. All arrays start at zero. Compute $c_0(m)$ on $$-R-K-5\le m\le H+K+5$$ and define, wherever these three entries are present, $$\label{cert:shift-array}
 c_1(m)=c_0(m+3)-c_0(m)+c_0(m-1).$$ These extra endpoint entries ensure that every subsequent use of $c_1$ lies in its defined range. The arrays approximate the sequences $C_0,C_1$ in the main text.

For a rational input $c$ let $g(c),h(c)$ be the following rounded cosine and sine values. The displayed loop fixes the order of the summation.

    x = [pi' c];  y = [x*x]
    t = 1;  u = x;  g = t;  h = u
    for j = 1,...,50:
        t = [-t*y/(2*j*(2*j-1))]
        u = [-u*y/(2*j*(2*j+1))]
        g = g+t;  h = h+u
    return (g,h)

Write $g_A(m)=g(c_A(m))$ and $h_A(m)=h(c_A(m))$.

The only expanding root close to the unit circle is $\alpha$ and its conjugate. To treat their long tail, set <a id="cert:weak-coefficients"></a>$$\label{cert:weak-coefficients}
 a=1/\alpha,\qquad u_0=-a/p'(\alpha),\qquad
 u_1=u_0(a^3-1+1/a).$$ Their contribution to $C_A(m)$ for $m\ge H$ is $u_Aa^m+\overline{u_Aa^m}$. Let $(a',u'_0)$ be the pair in <a href="#cert:base-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:base-pairs">[cert:base-pairs]</a> belonging to $z_\alpha$. Compute $$a'_0=1,\qquad a'_{n+1}=[a'a'_n]\quad(0\le n<K),\qquad
 u'_1=[u'_0(a'_3-1+[1/a'])].$$ The subscript in $a'_n$ is a power-array index.

For a single moment use $w=u'_A$ and $k=0$. For a paired moment of types $A,B\in\{0,1\}$, sign $\sigma\in\{1,-1\}$, and lag $0\le k\le K$, use $w=u'_A+\sigma[u'_Ba'_k]$. In either case the following calculation returns an approximate moment. It retains the finite factors $-R-k\le m<H$, omits the rapidly convergent negative tail, and treats the positive tail by summing the degree-$2$, $4$, and $6$ terms of $-\log\cos$ through geometric series. Their sum is the variable `pen`; the divisors $2,12,45$ are the corresponding Taylor denominators. To compute $e^{-\texttt{pen}}$, the algorithm first approximates $e^{-\texttt{pen}/32}$ and then squares five times. This tail value is computed first, and the finite factors are then multiplied in increasing order of $m$. Section <a href="#cert:error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:error">7.3</a> bounds all omissions and rounding errors.

    U = [w*a'_H]
    q_0 = 1;  q_i = [q_{i-1}*U]       for i = 1,...,6
    b_0 = 1;  b_i = [b_{i-1}*pi']     for i = 1,...,6
    pen = 0
    for d = 2,4,6:
        mom = 0
        for j = 0,...,d:
            num = [q_j*conj(q_{d-j})]
            den = 1-[a'_j*conj(a'_{d-j})]
            mom = mom+binomial(d,j)*Re([num/den])
        pen = pen+[b_d*mom/f_d]       (f_2,f_4,f_6) = (2,12,45)
    x = [-pen/32];  t = 1;  value = t
    for j = 1,...,40:
        t = [t*x/j];  value = value+t
    repeat five times: value = [value*value]
    for m = -R-k,...,H-1, in increasing order:
        v = g_A(m)                               (single)
        v = [g_A(m)*g_B(m+k)-sigma*h_A(m)*h_B(m+k)] (pair)
        value = [value*v]
    return value

Denote the outputs by $\widetilde P_A$ and $\widetilde P_{AB}^{\sigma}(k)$, respectively.

<a id="cert:weights"></a>
We use only lags $30\le k\le2000$. With $s=0$ for $\sigma=1$ and $s=1$ for $\sigma=-1$, put $r=\widetilde P_{00}^{\sigma}(k)$ and set $$\label{cert:weights}
 w_{ks}=\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(r)}{W}
 \left\lfloor .68W\max(0,|r|-.00026)+\frac12\right\rfloor,
 \qquad 30\le k\le2000.$$ Take $\operatorname{sgn}(0)=0$ and omit zero weights from subsequent sums. The recurrences above and this formula define the weights without any choices or numerical tolerances.

<a id="cert:error"></a>
#### A uniform error bound for the moment calculation

<a id="cert:delta"></a>
We prove $$\label{cert:delta}
 |\widetilde P_A-P_A|<\Delta,
 \qquad
 |\widetilde P_{AB}^{\sigma}(k)-P_{AB}^{\sigma}(k)|<\Delta,
 \qquad \Delta=5\cdot10^{-6},$$ for all types, signs, and lags used above. The important issue is the weakly contracting base $a=1/\alpha$: truncating its powers at $H=100$ would not be accurate. The geometric sums in the tail calculation retain all of these powers.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Coefficient and trigonometric errors.

<a id="cert:coefficient-errors"></a>
For $|z|<2.001$, termwise differentiation gives $$|p''(z)|<4\cdot10^{13}.$$ Hence $p'$ changes by less than $4\cdot10^{-22}$ between a center and its root. By <a href="#cert:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual">[cert:residual]</a>, reciprocal differentiation and rounding in <a href="#cert:base-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:base-pairs">[cert:base-pairs]</a> give base errors less than $2/S$ and coefficient errors less than $10^{-21}$. Direct squared modulus comparisons give $|a_z|<1$ and $|v_z|\le1$ for every stored pair. The corresponding exact coefficients have modulus less than $1.001$. Thus each recurrence step $v\leftarrow[va_z]$ propagates the existing error with factor at most $1$ and adds less than $4/S$. There are fewer than $5100$ such steps in any needed entry. Each coefficient-times-power term consequently has error less than $2\cdot10^{-21}$, and $$\label{cert:coefficient-errors}
 |c_0(m)-C_0(m)|<7\cdot10^{-20},\qquad
 |c_1(m)-C_1(m)|<2.1\cdot10^{-19}.$$

<a id="cert:array-bounds"></a>
The finite recurrence also gives the following rational comparisons: $$\label{cert:array-bounds}
 \begin{gathered}
 \max_{A,m}|c_A(m)|<.847,\qquad
 \max_{m\ge-20}|c_0(m)|<.018,\qquad
 \max_A|u'_A|<.0087,\\
 .8460<\max_{A,m}|c_A(m)|<.8462.
 \end{gathered}$$ The maxima here range over the computed entries; use squared moduli for the complex coefficients $u'_A$. To reproduce them, perform the coefficient recurrences in <a href="#cert:base-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:base-pairs">[cert:base-pairs]</a>, the additions in <a href="#cert:shift-array" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:shift-array">[cert:shift-array]</a>, and the displayed definition of $u'_1$. These comparisons require neither evaluation of trigonometric functions nor a root-finding routine.

Every later trigonometric input is a sum of at most four of these coefficients, counted with multiplicity. Its error is less than $10^{-18}$, and its rounded angle satisfies $|x|\le13$ and $|y|\le170$. The angle error is less than $4\cdot10^{-18}$. For the Taylor recurrences at a fixed value of $y$, each term-rounding error contributes at most $e^{14}/S$ to the final sum: the subsequent factorial denominators bound the amplification by $\sum_{j\ge0}170^j/(2j)!<e^{14}$. Replacing $x^2$ by $y$ costs at most $100e^{14}/S$, by differentiating the absolute term sums. The omitted Taylor tails at $|x|\le13$ are less than $10^{-30}$. Together these effects give errors below $10^{-20}$ relative to the computed angle, and below $10^{-16}$ relative to the true cosine and sine. In particular every single or paired finite factor has error less than $10^{-15}$.

There are at most $R+K+H=5100$ finite factors. The true factors have modulus at most $1$, and their approximations have modulus at most $1+10^{-15}$. Telescoping the product, including its roundings, shows that with an initial value of modulus at most $2$ these errors contribute less than $3\cdot10^{-11}$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Terms omitted at the two ends.

On the negative side, $|C_0(m)|\le32(.994)^{-1-m}$ for $m<0$, by summing the contracting-root terms. Including the three shifts for type $1$, a single or paired coefficient at $m<-R-k$ is bounded by $$200(.994)^{-4-(m+k)}.$$ Using $1-\cos u\le u^2/2$ and telescoping products, the total omitted product costs at most $$\frac{3.142^2\,200^2}{2}
 \frac{(.994)^{5994}}{1-(.994)^2}<5\cdot10^{-8}.$$ For example, the rational inequality $(.994)^{2996}<3\cdot10^{-8}$ already suffices for this estimate. On the positive side the only terms omitted from the coefficients are those from $\beta$. Their bases are less than $.545$, and their type-$0$ and type-$1$ coefficients have modulus at most $4$. The cosine Lipschitz bound therefore gives a total error at most $3.142\cdot8\cdot(.545)^{100}/(1-.545)<10^{-20}$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### The geometric tail and its rounding.

Let $w_*=u_A$ in the single case and $w_*=u_A+\sigma u_Ba^k$ in the paired case. Put $U_*=w_*a^H$. The exact sums of the even powers of the weak-root tail are <a id="cert:geometric-moments"></a>$$\label{cert:geometric-moments}
 \mu_d=\sum_{n\ge0}(U_*a^n+\overline{U_*a^n})^d
 =\sum_{j=0}^d\binom dj\Re
 \frac{U_*^j\overline{U_*}^{d-j}}{1-a^j\bar a^{d-j}}
 \quad(d=2,4,6).$$ The equality follows by the binomial theorem and absolutely convergent geometric series. In particular $\mu_d\ge0$.

<a id="cert:penalty"></a>
The power-array errors satisfy $|a'_n-a^n|\le3n/S$. Because $|a|,|a'|>.99$, the formula for $u'_1$ gives errors below $5\cdot10^{-21}$ for both $u'_A$, and $|u_A|\le.00871$. It follows that the computed $U$ has error below $10^{-19}$; its powers through degree $6$ have errors below $10^{-18}$. In each quotient in [(cert:penalty)](#cert:penalty) the algorithm, the numerator error is below $3\cdot10^{-18}$ and the denominator error is below $10^{-28}$. The true and computed denominators have modulus greater than $.00019$: use $$|1-a^j\bar a^{d-j}|\ge1-|a|^d
 >1-(.9999)^2>.00019$$ and the stated rounding error. Hence each quotient error is below $2\cdot10^{-14}$, the error in each computed $\mu_d$ is below $2\cdot10^{-12}$, and $|\mu_d|<4\cdot10^5$ by the same denominator bound. The rounded powers of $\pi'$ through degree $6$ have errors below $10^{-22}$. Consequently `pen` approximates the nonnegative number $$\label{cert:penalty}
 P=\frac{\pi^2\mu_2}{2}+\frac{\pi^4\mu_4}{12}
       +\frac{\pi^6\mu_6}{45}$$ to within $10^{-9}$.

For $m\ge H$, the weak coefficient has angle bounded by $$3.142\cdot4\cdot.00871\cdot(.9999)^m
 <.11(.9999)^m.$$ Summing the powers of this bound gives $$P\le\frac{.11^2}{2(1-.9999^2)}
      +\frac{.11^4}{12(1-.9999^4)}
      +\frac{.11^6}{45(1-.9999^6)}<31.$$ Thus $|\texttt{pen}|<32$ and $|x|<1.1$ in the exponential calculation. The $40$-term exponential recurrence has Taylor remainder less than $10^{-40}$. Its rounding errors, bounded using $e^{1.1}$, are negligible compared with $10^{-9}$. Each squaring amplifies its existing error by at most $3$, since the true intermediate values for the computed penalty are at most $e^{10^{-9}}$. Including the error in <a href="#cert:penalty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:penalty">[cert:penalty]</a>, the returned exponential is within $4\cdot10^{-9}$ of $e^{-P}$.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### The analytic remainder of the tail.

<a id="cert:logcos-remainder"></a>
For real $z$ with $|z|\le.11$ one has $$\label{cert:logcos-remainder}
 \left| -\log\cos z-\frac{z^2}{2}-\frac{z^4}{12}
                         -\frac{z^6}{45}\right|\le.1z^8.$$ Here are elementary bounds proving the stated constant. Set $y=z^2$, $h=1-\cos z$, and $h_0=y/2-y^2/24+y^3/720$. Then $0\le h\le y/2$ and $|h-h_0|\le y^4/40320$. In $-\log(1-h)=h+h^2/2+h^3/3+\cdots$, the terms after the third are bounded by $h^4/(4(1-h))<.016y^4$. Substitution of $h_0$ in the first three terms costs less than $.00004y^4$. The terms through degree $3$ are $y/2+y^2/12+y^3/45$; the sum of the absolute values of the remaining coefficients is less than $.03$, so their contribution is less than $.03y^4$ for $0\le y\le.0121$. These bounds imply <a href="#cert:logcos-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:logcos-remainder">[cert:logcos-remainder]</a>.

Applying <a href="#cert:logcos-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:logcos-remainder">[cert:logcos-remainder]</a> to all weak tail angles gives $$\left|\sum_{m\ge H}-\log\cos
       \bigl(\pi(w_*a^m+\overline{w_*a^m})\bigr)-P\right|
 \le\frac{.1(.11)^8}{1-(.9999)^8}<3\cdot10^{-6}.$$ Both penalties are nonnegative, so the same bound applies to the difference of their exponentials. Combining this with the rounding error, the finite-factor error, and the two omitted ends proves <a href="#cert:delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:delta">[cert:delta]</a>; indeed the sum of the stated errors is below $3.055\cdot10^{-6}$.

<a id="cert:moments"></a>
#### Correlation, variance, and the absolute Gram sum

<span id="cert:moment-proof" label="cert:moment-proof"></span>

For the rest of the appendix, abbreviate $Z_i=\mathcal Z_i^{(0)}$ and $G_i=\mathcal G_i$ from <a href="#np:stationary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:stationary">[np:stationary]</a> and <a href="#np:predictor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="np:predictor">[np:predictor]</a>. All expectations are under the stationary law of the two-sided independent bits.

The preceding calculation defines exact rational weights and bounds each moment error uniformly. We next sum those moments to prove the first three estimates of Proposition <a href="#np:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:moments">[np:moments]</a>. A final finite-product estimate will prove the fourth.

<a id="cert:corr-sum"></a>
<a id="cert:T-sum"></a>
Write $X=-.001$, $Y=.0055$, and let $\mathcal R$ be the nonzero rows $(w_{ks},k,s)$ with $30\le k\le2000$. Define $$b_A=\widetilde P_A,\qquad
 m_{k0}=\widetilde P_{00}^{+}(k),\quad
 m_{k1}=\widetilde P_{00}^{-}(k),\qquad
 e=\widetilde P_{01}^{+}(33).$$ The approximated correlation is $$\label{cert:corr-sum}
 \widetilde c=Xb_0+Ye+\sum_{(w,k,s)\in\mathcal R}wm_{ks}.$$ For each lag $k$, let $J_{k0}$ and $J_{k1}$ be respectively the second (minus) and first (plus) entries of the following pair: $$\begin{cases}
 (\widetilde P_{10}^{+}(k-33),\widetilde P_{10}^{-}(k-33)),&k\ge33,\\
 (\widetilde P_{01}^{+}(33-k),\widetilde P_{01}^{-}(33-k)),&k<33.
 \end{cases}$$ The approximated squared norm and absolute Gram sum are <a id="cert:var-sum"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \widetilde v={}&X^2+Y^2+2XYb_1
 +2\sum_{(w,k,s)\in\mathcal R}w(Xb_0+YJ_{ks})
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &+\sum_{(w,k,s),(v,l,t)\in\mathcal R}
           wv\,m_{|k-l|,\,\mathbf1_{s=t}},\label{cert:var-sum}\\
 \widetilde T={}&
 \sum_{\substack{(w,k,s),(v,l,t)\in\mathcal R\\k,l\ge50}}
       |wv\,m_{|k-l|,\,\mathbf1_{s=t}}|.\label{cert:T-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Both double sums are over ordered pairs. Equal signs require the minus moment in a conjugated phase pair; opposite signs require the plus moment. This explains the index $\mathbf1_{s=t}$, including the diagonal cases.

The exact finite calculations give <a id="cert:exact-output"></a>$$\label{cert:exact-output}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\mathcal R|&=753,\\
 |X|+|Y|+\sum_{\mathcal R}|w|&=198442946/W,\\
 |X|+|Y|+\sum_{\substack{(w,k,s)\in\mathcal R\\k<50}}|w|
     &=11816095/W,\\
 WS\widetilde c&=211815537786494774284357596751188225,\\
 W^2S\widetilde v&=143920884250294630908494564120361905955993456,\\
 W^2S\widetilde T&=105303788239450118397416374596041413014338334.
 \end{aligned}$$ These are integer equalities: compute the arrays and products in Section <a href="#cert:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:algorithm">7.2</a>, round the weights by <a href="#cert:weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:weights">[cert:weights]</a>, and use the unrounded rational sums <a href="#cert:corr-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:corr-sum">[cert:corr-sum]</a>–<a href="#cert:T-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:T-sum">[cert:T-sum]</a>. In particular $$211810<10^9\widetilde c<211820,\quad
 143910<10^9\widetilde v<143930,\quad
 105300<10^9\widetilde T<105310.$$ This gives $|G_i|<.199$ and $|G_i^{(N)}(x)|<.199$ by the triangle inequality. The total correlation error is at most $\Delta\cdot.199$, and the squared-norm error is at most $\Delta\cdot.199^2$. Thus $$\mathbb E\Re(Z_iG_i)>.0002108205>.000210,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E|G_i|^2<.000144119<.000145.$$

<a id="cert:T-bound"></a>
To prepare for the last estimate, call the constant row, the type-$1$ row, and the rows with $k<50$ the *small rows*; the remaining rows are the *big rows*. A row of weight $w_v$ has phase $U_v=\exp(\pi\mathrm i\sum_m f_v(m)e_m)$, where $e_m=2b_{i+m}-1$. Its sequence $f_v$ is zero for the constant, $C_1(m+33)$ for the type-$1$ row, and $(1-2s)C_0(m+k)$ for a row $(w,k,s)$. Define the true absolute Gram sum <a id="cert:true-T"></a>$$\label{cert:true-T}
 T=\sum_{v,v'\ {\rm big}}|w_vw_{v'}\mathbb E U_v\overline{U_{v'}}|.$$ The error between this sum and <a href="#cert:T-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:T-sum">[cert:T-sum]</a> is at most $\Delta\cdot.199^2$, by $||x|-|y||\le|x-y|$. Hence $$\label{cert:T-bound}
 T<.000116,\qquad \sqrt T<.011.$$ The total absolute weight of the small rows is less than $.012$.

<a id="cert:finite-products"></a>
#### Finite products controlling the remaining moment

<a id="cert:tangent-bound"></a>
We now bound $\mathbb E(Z_i^2G_i^2)$. The big rows have small coefficients on the $71$ positions $-70\le m\le0$. More precisely, with $t=.06$, <a href="#cert:coefficient-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:coefficient-errors">[cert:coefficient-errors]</a> and <a href="#cert:array-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:array-bounds">[cert:array-bounds]</a> give $$\label{cert:tangent-bound}
 |\tan(\pi f_v(m))|\le t
 \quad(v\hbox{ big},\ -70\le m\le0).$$ Indeed $m+k\ge-20$ for these rows, and the coefficient bound $.018$, together with $\sin u\le u$ and $\cos u\ge1-u^2/2$, is sufficient.

<a id="cert:B-bounds"></a>
For any real sequence $V$ put $c_m=|\cos(\pi V(m))|$ and $s_m=|\sin(\pi V(m))|$. The finite products needed below are <a id="cert:B-def"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 B_2(V)&=\prod_{m=-70}^0
  \min\left(1,\frac{(1+t^2)c_m+2ts_m}{1-t^2}\right),\notag\\
 B_1(V)&=\prod_{m=-70}^0
  \min\left(1,\frac{c_m+ts_m}{\sqrt{1-t^2}}\right),\label{cert:B-def}\\
 B_0(V)&=\prod_{m=-200}^0c_m.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Their bounds are $$\label{cert:B-bounds}
 B_2(2C_0)<.011,\qquad
 \max_{f\ {\rm small}}B_1(2C_0+f)<.015,\qquad
 \max_{f,g\ {\rm small}}B_0(2C_0+f+g)<.0032.$$ Here the maxima range over the actual phase sequences of the small rows. Besides the constant and the type-$1$ row, their eight nonzero rows are as follows: $$\begin{array}{c|rrrrrrrr}
 k&32&36&37&39&40&43&44&47\\
 s&0&1&0&1&0&0&1&1\\
 Ww_{ks}&-536932&1265024&331701&47119&1483338&436861&458216&756904
\end{array}$$

For completeness, the following upper-rounding calculation verifies all of <a href="#cert:B-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:B-bounds">[cert:B-bounds]</a>. For each of its three cases and each indicated choice of small rows, form the approximate sequence $V'$ by replacing $C_A$ by $c_A$. Initialize $v=1$. For increasing $m$ in the relevant interval, compute $$\begin{aligned}
 b&=|g(V'(m))|+10^{-15},& y&=|h(V'(m))|+10^{-15},\\
 F_0&=b,&
 F_1&=S^{-1}+[1.002(b+.06y)],\\
 &&F_2&=S^{-1}+[1.004(1.0036b+.12y)],\\
 v&\leftarrow S^{-1}+[v\min(1,F_j)]&&\text{in case }j.
\end{aligned}$$ The factors $1.002$ and $1.004$ bound $(1-t^2)^{-1/2}$ and $(1-t^2)^{-1}$, respectively. The added $10^{-15}$ exceeds the trigonometric error proved above, and each added $S^{-1}$ compensates for a possibly downward rounding. Thus this calculation gives upper bounds, including for $B_0$ because its true factors are at most $1$. The maximum final numerators at scale $S$, in the order $B_2,B_1,B_0$, are <a id="cert:B-output"></a>$$\label{cert:B-output}
 \begin{gathered}
 10861284406435197401851134503,\\
 14731866010915969694738277129,\\
 3155364376927094122833206055.
 \end{gathered}$$ Multiplication by $10^6/S$ bounds them respectively by $10862$, $14732$, and $3156$, proving <a href="#cert:B-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:B-bounds">[cert:B-bounds]</a>.

<a id="cert:walsh"></a>
#### From the finite products to the anisotropy bound

<a id="cert:walsh-expansion"></a>
The estimates just obtained concern individual coefficients and finite products. We finish by showing how they control the full sum of phases without expanding every three-phase moment. Write $G_i=G_{\rm small}+G_{\rm big}$ according to the preceding partition, and fix any subset $J\subset\{-70,\ldots,0\}$. Expanding the big phases in the independent signs $e_m$ on $J$ gives $$\label{cert:walsh-expansion}
 G_{\rm big}=\sum_{A\subset J}\mathrm i^{|A|}e_AH_A,
 \qquad e_A=\prod_{m\in A}e_m,$$ where $$H_A=\sum_{v\ {\rm big}}w_vU_v^{\rm out}
       \prod_{m\in J}\cos(\pi f_v(m))
       \prod_{m\in A}\tan(\pi f_v(m)).$$ The phase $U_v^{\rm out}$ depends only on signs outside $J$, so each $H_A$ is independent of the signs on $J$.

<a id="cert:H-bound"></a>
We claim that $$\label{cert:H-bound}
 \|H_A\|_2\le\sqrt T\,t^{|A|}(1-t^2)^{-|J|/2}.$$ For two big rows, independence gives their full Gram entry as the outside Gram entry times $$\prod_{m\in J}\cos(\pi f_v(m))\cos(\pi f_{v'}(m))
       \bigl(1+\tan(\pi f_v(m))\tan(\pi f_{v'}(m))\bigr).$$ By <a href="#cert:tangent-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:tangent-bound">[cert:tangent-bound]</a>, each last factor is at least $1-t^2$. In the expansion of $\|H_A\|_2^2$, the extra tangent products have modulus at most $t^{2|A|}$. Taking absolute values term by term therefore bounds the squared norm by $Tt^{2|A|}(1-t^2)^{-|J|}$, proving <a href="#cert:H-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:H-bound">[cert:H-bound]</a>.

First consider $\mathbb E(Z_i^2G_{\rm big}^2)$ and set $V=2C_0$. For a pair $A,A'\subset J$ in <a href="#cert:walsh-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:walsh-expansion">[cert:walsh-expansion]</a>, averaging the signs on $J$ contributes a factor of modulus $c_m$ where membership in $A,A'$ agrees, and a factor of modulus $s_m$ where it differs. The outside expectation is bounded by $\|H_A\|_2\|H_{A'}\|_2$ using Cauchy–Schwarz and the unit modulus of the outside part of $Z_i^2$. Summing over the four membership choices at each site gives $$|\mathbb E(Z_i^2G_{\rm big}^2)|
 \le T\prod_{m\in J}\frac{(1+t^2)c_m+2ts_m}{1-t^2}.$$ Choose $J$ to contain precisely those positions whose displayed factor is less than $1$. This yields <a id="cert:big-square"></a>$$\label{cert:big-square}
 |\mathbb E(Z_i^2G_{\rm big}^2)|\le T B_2(2C_0).$$

For a fixed small phase $U$ with sequence $f$, use the same expansion with $V=2C_0+f$. There is now one subset $A$; summing its two membership choices produces $c_m+ts_m$. The outside expectation is bounded by $\|H_A\|_2$. Optimizing $J$ as before gives $$|\mathbb E(Z_i^2UG_{\rm big})|
 \le\sqrt T B_1(2C_0+f).$$ For two small phases with sequences $f,g$, independence directly bounds their full moment by $B_0(2C_0+f+g)$: discard all the other absolute cosine factors, which are at most $1$.

Finally sum these bounds with the absolute row weights. Using <a href="#cert:T-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:T-bound">[cert:T-bound]</a>, <a href="#cert:B-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:B-bounds">[cert:B-bounds]</a>, and the small-weight bound $.012$, we obtain $$\begin{split}
 |\mathbb E(Z_i^2G_i^2)|
 &\le T\cdot.011+2(.012)\sqrt T\cdot.015
                           +(.012)^2\cdot.0032\\
 &<.000116\cdot.011+2\cdot.012\cdot.011\cdot.015
                           +.012^2\cdot.0032\\
 &=.0000056968<.000007.
 \end{split}$$ This proves the remaining estimate of Proposition <a href="#np:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="np:moments">[np:moments]</a>.
